03Reservations, Overbooking, and WalksThe booked room type is unavailable+

Your goal

Present verified alternatives, explain differences honestly, and obtain a guest decision before changing the booking.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

The booked room type is unavailable

Mr Chen booked one king-bed room for tonight. That room type is unavailable. Duty manager Sofia has two verified alternatives: an on-site twin room with two separate beds at no additional charge, or a king-bed room at the nearby Harbor Hotel. For the off-site option, this property has approved payment of the room-price difference and a transfer; other expenses are not approved. Harbor Hotel has confirmed availability for tonight. Mr Chen has not selected an option. Sofia must explain the bed arrangements and location, check his preference, and complete the chosen arrangement before calling it confirmed.

Sofia: Duty manager / Mr Chen: Guest with a king-room booking

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which alternative preserves the booked bed arrangement?

Answer and explanation

B. The king room at Harbor Hotel

Harbor Hotel has the verified king-bed alternative; the on-site room has two separate beds.

2. What costs are approved for the off-site option?

Answer and explanation

D. The room-price difference and transfer

The brief limits the approved package to the room-price difference and transfer.

3. Has the guest accepted an alternative?

Answer and explanation

C. No; his choice is still required.

Both alternatives are available, but the guest has not selected one.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A twin room does not preserve a king-bed configuration. A king room at another hotel preserves that feature but changes location. Neither alternative should be called identical to the original booking.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
room type
A category of accommodation with specified features.verify the room type
king room
A room containing a king-size bed.confirm a king room
twin room
A room with two separate beds.offer a twin room
bed configuration
The number and arrangement of beds.explain the bed configuration
overbooking
Accepting more bookings than the available capacity can accommodate.manage an overbooking
room-type mismatch
A difference between booked and offered accommodation categories.resolve a room-type mismatch
walk
Industry shorthand for relocating a guest to another property.arrange a guest walk
relocation
Moving a booking or guest to another property.explain the relocation
alternative accommodation
A substitute place to stay.verify alternative accommodation
receiving property
The property accepting a relocated guest.contact the receiving property
on-site
Located at the same property.offer an on-site alternative
off-site
Located away from the original property.arrange an off-site room
availability check
Verification that a room or service can be supplied.complete an availability check
rate difference
The difference between the two room prices.cover the rate difference
transfer
Transport between locations.arrange the approved transfer
incidental expense
An additional cost outside the basic room arrangement.clarify incidental expenses
approved allowance
A specifically authorized amount or type of support.state the approved allowance
guest consent
The guest's agreement to the proposed arrangement.obtain guest consent
booking amendment
A documented change to an existing reservation.confirm a booking amendment
confirmation number
An identifier for a confirmed booking.verify the confirmation number
arrival instructions
Directions for reaching or entering the receiving property.provide arrival instructions
handover contact
The person coordinating the receiving end of an arrangement.identify the handover contact
service commitment
A specific promise made about the service provided.document the service commitment
equivalent feature
A characteristic that genuinely matches the original requirement.check equivalent features

Availability, guest agreement, and completion of the replacement booking are separate stages. Approval to cover a price difference and transfer is not an unlimited promise to pay all incidental expenses.

C Notice the language

A substitute is not automatically equivalent

Two available rooms may differ in the feature that matters most to a guest. Describe beds, location, and approved costs plainly. Internal shorthand such as walk should not replace a respectful explanation of the actual accommodation arrangement.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the problem directly
Your booked king-room type is unavailable tonight.
Acknowledge responsibility
I am sorry we cannot provide the room type you reserved.
Introduce choices
I have two verified alternatives to explain.
Describe the on-site option
We have a twin room here with two separate beds.
Clarify the charge
There would be no additional room charge for that option.
Describe the off-site option
Harbor Hotel has confirmed a king room for tonight.
Name the difference
That keeps the bed type but changes the location.
Explain approved costs
We will cover the room-price difference and the transfer.
Bound the offer
Other expenses are not included in the approved arrangement.
Ask what matters
Is the king bed or staying at this property more important?
Avoid assuming agreement
I have not changed your booking without your choice.
Check the selection
Would you like me to arrange the Harbor Hotel option?
Complete the handover
I will confirm the booking and arrival details.
Keep timing accurate
I still need to confirm the transfer arrangements.
Use plain language
We can arrange accommodation at the nearby hotel.
Close with a readback
Let me repeat the room, location, and costs we have agreed.
6 language and precision notes
Available
Does not mean already booked in this guest's name.
Included
Specify exactly what the package includes.
Equivalent
Avoid when a material feature or location differs.
Walk
Internal jargon; explain relocation plainly to the guest.
Would you like
Requests a choice rather than assuming agreement.
Confirmed
Identify which part of the arrangement is confirmed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which description is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. The twin room is on-site but has two separate beds.

The correct description preserves the verified difference in bed configuration.

2. Which statement overpromises?

Answer and explanation

B. We will pay every expense you incur.

The approval does not extend to every incidental or future expense.

3. What should happen before changing the booking?

Answer and explanation

A. Explain the alternatives and obtain the guest's choice.

The guest has not selected an alternative and needs accurate information before agreeing.

4. Which handover is complete in principle?

Answer and explanation

B. Verify the chosen booking, receiving contact, arrival details, and approved transfer.

A practical relocation requires coordination of the selected accommodation and its agreed supporting arrangements.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

The booked room type is unavailable

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sofia: Duty manager / Mr Chen: Guest with a king-room booking

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • transfer
  • arrival instructions
  • readback
  • consent
  • receiving property
  • rate difference
  • room type
  • twin room
  • relocation
  • booking amendment
  1. Mr Chen
    I reserved a king room; two separate beds do not suit us. The receptionist mentioned a problem. What is available?
  2. Sofia
    I am sorry. Your booked 1 is unavailable tonight. I have two verified alternatives, and I want to explain the differences before you choose either one.
  3. Mr Chen
    Please start with the option here. We would prefer not to move unless staying means losing the bed arrangement that we specifically booked.
  4. Sofia
    The on-site option is a 2 with two separate beds, at no additional charge. It keeps you at this property, but it does not match your king-bed preference.
  5. Mr Chen
    That difference matters to us. What is the second option, and have you actually checked that the other hotel can provide the right room tonight?
  6. Sofia
    The 3 is Harbor Hotel nearby. They have confirmed that a king room is available tonight. That preserves the bed type, although it changes where you will stay.
  7. Mr Chen
    Would I have to pay the difference? I do not want to arrive there and discover that this solution costs more than the booking I made.
  8. Sofia
    Our approved arrangement covers the 4 and the transfer to Harbor Hotel. Other expenses are not included. I will make that scope clear in the arrangements rather than leave you with an open-ended promise.
  9. Mr Chen
    The king room sounds better for us. Before you arrange it, please confirm we would not pay extra for the room or the transfer.
  10. Sofia
    Yes. I need your 5 before completing the change. I have not treated the availability check as your acceptance or amended the booking without asking you.
  11. Mr Chen
    Then we choose the king room at Harbor Hotel for tonight. Please confirm the actual reservation before we leave with the luggage.
  12. Sofia
    I will complete the 6 and coordinate with Harbor Hotel. I will check the room, arrival details, and payment arrangements so the receiving team has the same information.
  13. Mr Chen
    A member of staff used the word walk earlier. Does that mean we are expected to walk there with our bags? That would be difficult.
  14. Sofia
    No. That is internal shorthand for 7. In your case, the approved offer includes transport. I should have explained the arrangement in ordinary language instead of leaving that term unclear.
  15. Mr Chen
    Do you have a transport departure time yet? Should we stay by reception or wait somewhere else?
  16. Sofia
    I still need to confirm the 8 details. Please remain by reception while I coordinate them. I will not describe a departure time as confirmed until the transport arrangement is verified.
  17. Mr Chen
    That is fine. Before we leave, please give us the receiving contact and explain what we should show when we arrive at Harbor Hotel.
  18. Sofia
    I will provide the 9 once the booking is completed. They will identify the receiving contact and booking details, with the agreed room-price and transfer arrangements clearly recorded.
  19. Mr Chen
    Could you put those details in writing? I do not want to explain the price and transport agreement again at Harbor Hotel.
  20. Sofia
    Certainly. First, a quick 10: a king room at Harbor Hotel tonight, price difference and transfer covered, other expenses excluded. I will complete the arrangements and confirm them in writing before you leave.

Answers and explanations
  1. room type Turn 2. Room type identifies the accommodation category that cannot be supplied tonight, rather than suggesting the entire reservation never existed.
  2. twin room Turn 4. A twin room has two separate beds and therefore does not preserve the guest's booked king-bed arrangement.
  3. receiving property Turn 6. The receiving property is Harbor Hotel, which has verified the relevant accommodation for tonight.
  4. rate difference Turn 8. The rate difference is the additional room-price amount the original property has specifically approved paying.
  5. consent Turn 10. Consent means the guest agrees to the specific alternative after hearing the differences and approved costs.
  6. booking amendment Turn 12. The booking amendment records the guest's selected change; it is distinct from merely identifying an available alternative.
  7. relocation Turn 14. Relocation means accommodation at another property; the approved transfer means the guest is not simply told to walk there.
  8. transfer Turn 16. The transfer is the transport arrangement, which requires its own confirmation even though the room option is available.
  9. arrival instructions Turn 18. Arrival instructions make the receiving handover practical by explaining where to go and what booking information is needed.
  10. readback Turn 20. A readback repeats the agreed details so both parties can correct any misunderstanding before the relocation is carried out.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr Chen: I reserved a king room; two separate beds do not suit us. The receptionist mentioned a problem. What is available?
  2. Sofia: I am sorry. Your booked room type is unavailable tonight. I have two verified alternatives, and I want to explain the differences before you choose either one.
  3. Mr Chen: Please start with the option here. We would prefer not to move unless staying means losing the bed arrangement that we specifically booked.
  4. Sofia: The on-site option is a twin room with two separate beds, at no additional charge. It keeps you at this property, but it does not match your king-bed preference.
  5. Mr Chen: That difference matters to us. What is the second option, and have you actually checked that the other hotel can provide the right room tonight?
  6. Sofia: The receiving property is Harbor Hotel nearby. They have confirmed that a king room is available tonight. That preserves the bed type, although it changes where you will stay.
  7. Mr Chen: Would I have to pay the difference? I do not want to arrive there and discover that this solution costs more than the booking I made.
  8. Sofia: Our approved arrangement covers the rate difference and the transfer to Harbor Hotel. Other expenses are not included. I will make that scope clear in the arrangements rather than leave you with an open-ended promise.
  9. Mr Chen: The king room sounds better for us. Before you arrange it, please confirm we would not pay extra for the room or the transfer.
  10. Sofia: Yes. I need your consent before completing the change. I have not treated the availability check as your acceptance or amended the booking without asking you.
  11. Mr Chen: Then we choose the king room at Harbor Hotel for tonight. Please confirm the actual reservation before we leave with the luggage.
  12. Sofia: I will complete the booking amendment and coordinate with Harbor Hotel. I will check the room, arrival details, and payment arrangements so the receiving team has the same information.
  13. Mr Chen: A member of staff used the word walk earlier. Does that mean we are expected to walk there with our bags? That would be difficult.
  14. Sofia: No. That is internal shorthand for relocation. In your case, the approved offer includes transport. I should have explained the arrangement in ordinary language instead of leaving that term unclear.
  15. Mr Chen: Do you have a transport departure time yet? Should we stay by reception or wait somewhere else?
  16. Sofia: I still need to confirm the transfer details. Please remain by reception while I coordinate them. I will not describe a departure time as confirmed until the transport arrangement is verified.
  17. Mr Chen: That is fine. Before we leave, please give us the receiving contact and explain what we should show when we arrive at Harbor Hotel.
  18. Sofia: I will provide the arrival instructions once the booking is completed. They will identify the receiving contact and booking details, with the agreed room-price and transfer arrangements clearly recorded.
  19. Mr Chen: Could you put those details in writing? I do not want to explain the price and transport agreement again at Harbor Hotel.
  20. Sofia: Certainly. First, a quick readback: a king room at Harbor Hotel tonight, price difference and transfer covered, other expenses excluded. I will complete the arrangements and confirm them in writing before you leave.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Twin rooms or a partner property

Fictional overbooking case: a hotel authorizes either two twin rooms onsite or two doubles at a partner property for one night at the booked total; transport is not yet arranged.

  1. Reservations manager: Our overbooking means the two doubles are unavailable tonight. We have two approved alternatives.
  2. Tour coordinator: The travelers need separate rooms, but they strongly prefer the double beds they booked.
  3. Reservations manager: They can take two twins here or two doubles at our partner property tonight.
  4. Tour coordinator: Would a walk to the partner property change the total accommodation charge for them?
  5. Reservations manager: For this arrangement, the booked total stays the same. Transport still needs arranging.
  6. Tour coordinator: Then I can't accept it until I know how they'll reach the other hotel.
  7. Reservations manager: Understood. If the transfer arrangements suit them, we can proceed with that alternative.
  8. Tour coordinator: Please check transport first. I won't announce a confirmed move while that remains open.
  9. Reservations manager: I'll coordinate the transfer request and keep the onsite twin option clear in our discussion.
  10. Tour coordinator: Once you have those details, I'll ask the travelers which tradeoff they prefer.
Keeping a three-night stay together

Separate fictional case: a group can book six standard rooms for three nights or six superior rooms for two nights followed by a move to standard rooms; quoted totals are equal.

  1. Group sales agent: We can keep six standard rooms for all three nights, or offer superior rooms initially.
  2. Event planner: Initially sounds uncertain. How many nights would those superior rooms actually cover for this group?
  3. Group sales agent: Two nights, followed by a move to standard rooms. Both quoted totals are equal.
  4. Event planner: Our final morning is busy. Can you guarantee the move won't interrupt the program?
  5. Group sales agent: I can't guarantee that. Staying in standard rooms avoids that planned room change entirely.
  6. Event planner: The upgrade is attractive, but moving luggage matters more than the larger room size.
  7. Group sales agent: Then the standard allocation may fit better, provided your delegates accept that room category.
  8. Event planner: I'll take that choice to them. Please don't release the booking as superior rooms.
  9. Group sales agent: I'll record the proposed standard allocation and request your decision before confirming the rooming list.
  10. Event planner: Good. I'll explain the room-category and room-change tradeoff instead of describing the options as identical.
Comparing channel offers fairly

Separate fictional reservation case: a direct offer includes breakfast and flexible cancellation; an agency offer is room only with different cancellation terms. No matched rate has been authorized.

  1. Corporate travel buyer: The agency rate is lower. Can you match it and keep your direct-booking benefits?
  2. Revenue coordinator: The offers differ: ours includes breakfast and flexible cancellation; the agency's is room only.
  3. Corporate travel buyer: I thought rate parity meant the same room always had exactly the same price.
  4. Revenue coordinator: We need to compare the actual offers and terms, not assume those packages are equivalent.
  5. Corporate travel buyer: Our travelers need breakfast, but their dates are fixed. Could you quote that combination?
  6. Revenue coordinator: I can request a separate quote. I can't confirm a matched rate without approval.
  7. Corporate travel buyer: Then give me the existing choices while the tailored quote is under review.
  8. Revenue coordinator: You can consider the flexible breakfast package or the agency's room-only offer under its conditions.
  9. Corporate travel buyer: I'll compare the full stay cost and cancellation terms before asking anyone to book.
  10. Revenue coordinator: I'll identify the pending quote clearly so it isn't mistaken for an approved reservation offer.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The booked room type is unavailable
  1. Read turns 1-8, contrasting two separate beds with a king bed without calling them equivalent.
  2. Repeat turns 13-20, explaining walk in ordinary language and reading back the included costs.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A feature match still needs agreement

A guest booked a king bed. The local room has two singles. A nearby hotel has a verified king room; the approved offer covers its price difference and transport only.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Agent: "The local option has a different ___."

Answer and explanation

B. bed configuration

Two singles differ from the king bed originally booked.

2. Guest: "The nearby option preserves the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. king bed

The verified nearby room contains the requested king bed.

3. Agent: "Our offer includes the price difference and ___."

Answer and explanation

B. transport

These are the two costs explicitly approved in the scenario.

4. Guest: "Please confirm the details before changing my ___."

Answer and explanation

B. booking

A change should follow informed agreement and completed coordination.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Reservations, Overbooking, and Walks
Original fictional case: Mr Chen booked one king-bed room for tonight. That room type is unavailable. Duty manager Sofia has two verified alternatives: an on-site twin room with two separate beds at no additional charge, or a king-bed room at the nearby Harbor Hotel. For the off-site option, this property has approved payment of the room-price difference and a transfer; other expenses are not approved. Harbor Hotel has confirmed availability for tonight. Mr Chen has not selected an option. Sofia must explain the bed arrangements and location, check his preference, and complete the chosen arrangement before calling it confirmed.
Language workshop: The booked room type is unavailable
Communication goal: Present verified alternatives, explain differences honestly, and obtain a guest decision before changing the booking.
Language guidance: A twin room does not preserve a king-bed configuration. A king room at another hotel preserves that feature but changes location. Neither alternative should be called identical to the original booking.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Your booked king-room type is unavailable tonight. / I am sorry we cannot provide the room type you reserved. / I have two verified alternatives to explain. / We have a twin room here with two separate beds. / There would be no additional room charge for that option. / Harbor Hotel has confirmed a king room for tonight. / That keeps the bed type but changes the location. / We will cover the room-price difference and the transfer. / Other expenses are not included in the approved arrangement. / Is the king bed or staying at this property more important? / I have not changed your booking without your choice. / Would you like me to arrange the Harbor Hotel option? / I will confirm the booking and arrival details. / I still need to confirm the transfer arrangements. / We can arrange accommodation at the nearby hotel. / Let me repeat the room, location, and costs we have agreed.
Vocabulary: room type: A category of accommodation with specified features. / king room: A room containing a king-size bed. / twin room: A room with two separate beds. / bed configuration: The number and arrangement of beds. / overbooking: Accepting more bookings than the available capacity can accommodate. / room-type mismatch: A difference between booked and offered accommodation categories. / walk: Industry shorthand for relocating a guest to another property. / relocation: Moving a booking or guest to another property. / alternative accommodation: A substitute place to stay. / receiving property: The property accepting a relocated guest. / on-site: Located at the same property. / off-site: Located away from the original property. / availability check: Verification that a room or service can be supplied. / rate difference: The difference between the two room prices. / transfer: Transport between locations. / incidental expense: An additional cost outside the basic room arrangement. / approved allowance: A specifically authorized amount or type of support. / guest consent: The guest's agreement to the proposed arrangement. / booking amendment: A documented change to an existing reservation. / confirmation number: An identifier for a confirmed booking. / arrival instructions: Directions for reaching or entering the receiving property. / handover contact: The person coordinating the receiving end of an arrangement. / service commitment: A specific promise made about the service provided. / equivalent feature: A characteristic that genuinely matches the original requirement.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8, contrasting two separate beds with a king bed without calling them equivalent. Repeat turns 13-20, explaining walk in ordinary language and reading back the included costs. Switch roles for the transfer and emphasize the feature that each option preserves or changes.
Partner: Sofia: Duty manager / Mr Chen: Guest with a king-room booking
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A guest booked a king bed. The local room has two singles. A nearby hotel has a verified king room; the approved offer covers its price difference and transport only.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr Chen: That is fine. Before we leave, please give us the receiving contact and explain what we should show when we arrive at Harbor Hotel.
Sofia: I will provide the arrival instructions once the booking is completed. They will identify the receiving contact and booking details, with the agreed room-price and transfer arrangements clearly recorded.
Mr Chen: Could you put those details in writing? I do not want to explain the price and transport agreement again at Harbor Hotel.
Sofia: Certainly. First, a quick readback: a king room at Harbor Hotel tonight, price difference and transfer covered, other expenses excluded. I will complete the arrangements and confirm them in writing before you leave.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Revenue Management and PricingA higher room rate did not produce higher room revenue+

Your goal

Explain occupancy, average daily rate, and revenue per available room using the correct denominators.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A higher room rate did not produce higher room revenue

Revenue analyst Mina reviews two single-night results with general manager Owen. Each night had 100 available rooms. On Night A, 80 rooms sold and room revenue was $8,000. On Night B, 60 rooms sold and room revenue was $7,200. These figures exclude non-room revenue; no cost figures are supplied. Owen calls the higher average daily rate a complete commercial improvement. Mina needs to distinguish the higher rate among sold rooms from the lower occupancy and lower revenue per available room. The two observations alone do not establish why demand changed.

Mina: Revenue analyst / Owen: General manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Night B's average daily rate?

Answer and explanation

A. $120

Room revenue of $7,200 divided by sixty sold rooms equals $120.

2. What is Night B's revenue per available room?

Answer and explanation

B. $72

The denominator is all one hundred available rooms, giving seventy-two dollars.

3. What cannot be calculated from the supplied figures?

Answer and explanation

A. Profit

Costs are not supplied, so room revenue measures do not establish profit.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Night A: occupancy 80%, ADR $100, RevPAR $80. Night B: occupancy 60%, ADR $120, RevPAR $72. ADR increased 20%, but RevPAR and total room revenue each decreased 10%. Occupancy fell 20 percentage points.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
average daily rate (ADR)
Room revenue divided by rooms sold for the period.calculate average daily rate
occupancy rate
Rooms sold as a percentage of rooms available.report the occupancy rate
revenue per available room (RevPAR)
Room revenue divided by available room supply.compare revenue per available room
room revenue
Revenue from selling guest rooms.distinguish room revenue
rooms sold
The count of paid room units sold in the stated period.verify rooms sold
rooms available
Room supply available for the stated reporting period.state rooms available
room night
One room for one night.measure demand in room nights
non-room revenue
Revenue from services other than room sales.separate non-room revenue
revenue management
Coordinating price and availability to manage revenue.review revenue management
rate strategy
The approach used to set and vary selling prices.evaluate the rate strategy
demand
Customer willingness and ability to purchase accommodation.assess room demand
booking pace
The speed at which reservations accumulate for a date.track booking pace
pickup
The change in booked business between reporting points.report booking pickup
on-the-books
Reservations already recorded for a future period.review on-the-books revenue
forecast occupancy
The estimated share of rooms expected to sell.revise forecast occupancy
market segment
A defined customer or business category.compare market segments
channel mix
The proportions sold through different booking routes.examine the channel mix
distribution cost
The expense associated with selling through a channel.include distribution costs
net room revenue
Room revenue after the specified deductions.define net room revenue
gross operating profit
Operating revenue less the relevant operating expenses.distinguish gross operating profit
competitive set
A selected group of properties used for comparison.review the competitive set
rate premium
A price above a stated comparison rate.explain the rate premium
price elasticity
The responsiveness of demand to price changes.assess price elasticity
revenue trade-off
A gain in one revenue driver offset by another change.explain a revenue trade-off

ADR uses rooms sold; RevPAR uses rooms available. The identical supply makes the revenue comparison straightforward here. Neither room metric includes all hotel revenue or costs, and two nights do not isolate the cause of a demand change.

C Notice the language

Challenge the measure, not the person

A manager may use a familiar headline as shorthand for success. Name the calculation and its boundary before disagreeing with the conclusion. A precise comparison lets the team discuss pricing without turning a useful challenge into a contest over confidence.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the headline
Which measure do we mean by improved?
State the common base
Both nights had 100 available rooms.
Give the first result
Night A sold 80 rooms for $8,000.
Give the second result
Night B sold 60 rooms for $7,200.
Explain ADR
Divide room revenue by rooms sold.
Report the rate change
ADR rose from $100 to $120.
Explain occupancy
Sixty of one hundred rooms sold, or 60%.
Use points correctly
Occupancy fell by 20 percentage points.
Explain RevPAR
Divide room revenue by rooms available.
Report the capacity yield
RevPAR fell from $80 to $72.
State the revenue change
Room revenue decreased by $800, or 10%.
Avoid a profit claim
We do not have the costs needed to calculate profit.
Keep the scope clear
These figures concern rooms, not all hotel revenue.
Avoid causal overreach
This comparison does not isolate the effect of pricing.
Request useful context
Let us examine demand, segments, and channel mix.
Summarize the trade-off
A higher sold-room rate coincided with fewer rooms sold.
6 language and precision notes
Per sold room
Names the ADR denominator, not total room supply.
Per available room
Includes the whole defined supply in the denominator.
Rose by versus rose to
By gives the change; to gives the resulting value.
Percentage points
Use for subtracting the two occupancy percentages.
Revenue versus profit
Revenue alone does not account for the relevant costs.
Coincided with
Reports a relationship without declaring its cause.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. ADR rose while occupancy, RevPAR, and room revenue fell.

The supplied calculations show higher sold-room rate but lower volume and capacity-based revenue.

2. Which calculation gives Night A's RevPAR?

Answer and explanation

A. $8,000 divided by 100

RevPAR divides room revenue by available room supply, not sold rooms.

3. Which occupancy change is correctly stated?

Answer and explanation

C. A fall of 20 percentage points

Eighty percent minus sixty percent is twenty points; the relative fall is twenty-five percent.

4. Which causal claim is justified by these two nights alone?

Answer and explanation

B. None; further evidence is needed to isolate a pricing effect.

The comparison reports outcomes but does not control other influences on demand.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

A higher room rate did not produce higher room revenue

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mina: Revenue analyst / Owen: General manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • rooms available
  • non-room revenue
  • ADR
  • trade-off
  • price elasticity
  • occupancy
  • channel mix
  • RevPAR
  • cost figures
  • room revenue
  1. Owen
    Our average rate went up on the second night, so I have called it a complete commercial improvement. Is there anything missing from that summary?
  2. Mina
    The higher 1 is real, but it is only one measure. We should look at how many rooms sold and what the available room supply earned as well.
  3. Owen
    Start with the room supply. I do not want to mistake a capacity change for performance.
  4. Mina
    Both nights had one hundred 2. Night A sold eighty rooms for eight thousand dollars. Night B sold sixty rooms for seven thousand two hundred dollars. These are room-only revenue figures.
  5. Owen
    So the first average is one hundred dollars per sold room, and the second is one hundred and twenty. That is a twenty-percent increase.
  6. Mina
    Correct. Meanwhile, 3 moved from eighty percent to sixty percent. That is a fall of twenty percentage points, or twenty-five percent relative to the original eighty-percent level.
  7. Owen
    I see the distinction. The higher rate applies to a smaller number of sold rooms. How should we describe the result across all available rooms?
  8. Mina
    Use 4. Eight thousand divided by one hundred is eighty dollars. Seven thousand two hundred divided by one hundred is seventy-two dollars. The capacity-based room revenue measure fell.
  9. Owen
    That means an eight-dollar reduction per available room. Relative to eighty dollars, it is ten percent lower, despite the higher average selling rate.
  10. Mina
    Yes. Total 5 also fell ten percent, from eight thousand to seven thousand two hundred. The unchanged supply means those two percentage changes align in this comparison.
  11. Owen
    Could we still say that profit improved because we had fewer rooms to service? Fewer occupied rooms might mean some costs were lower.
  12. Mina
    That is possible, but we have no 6 here. We cannot calculate profit or assume how expenses changed. A possible cost effect should remain a question, not become a reported financial result.
  13. Owen
    Fair point. What about restaurant and event spending? Those could change the property's total revenue even when the room figures move in the opposite direction.
  14. Mina
    They could, but 7 is not included in this comparison. We should label the scope clearly instead of using the room result as a complete measure of hotel performance.
  15. Owen
    Can I say the price increase caused the occupancy fall, or do we need to look at the business mix first?
  16. Mina
    We need more evidence. We have not established 8 or controlled other influences on demand. Let us compare the dates, segments, and booking patterns before attributing the whole decline to price.
  17. Owen
    Then the next discussion should look at context rather than force a cause into the headline. Which breakdown would make that review more useful?
  18. Mina
    Examine market segments and 9, alongside demand and booking pace. Keep the same period and definitions, and include relevant costs before making a profitability claim.
  19. Owen
    I will revise the summary to say that ADR increased while occupancy, RevPAR, and room revenue declined. That is less sweeping and more informative.
  20. Mina
    Exactly. It describes the observed 10 without treating one favorable metric as the whole result. We can then investigate why it happened and whether the strategy should change.

Answers and explanations
  1. ADR Turn 2. ADR measures average room revenue among sold rooms, so it can rise while overall room revenue falls.
  2. rooms available Turn 4. Rooms available is the common supply denominator, which stayed at one hundred on both nights.
  3. occupancy Turn 6. Occupancy is rooms sold divided by available rooms, giving eighty percent on Night A and sixty percent on Night B.
  4. RevPAR Turn 8. RevPAR uses all available rooms as the denominator, producing eighty dollars on Night A and seventy-two on Night B.
  5. room revenue Turn 10. Room revenue fell from eight thousand to seven thousand two hundred dollars, the same ten-percent decline with unchanged supply.
  6. cost figures Turn 12. Cost figures are needed before a revenue comparison can support a conclusion about profit.
  7. non-room revenue Turn 14. Non-room revenue is outside the supplied room-only figures, so no total-property revenue conclusion is established.
  8. price elasticity Turn 16. Price elasticity concerns how demand responds to price; two uncontrolled nights do not establish that response.
  9. channel mix Turn 18. Channel mix identifies how sales are distributed across booking routes and can add context to revenue and distribution-cost analysis.
  10. trade-off Turn 20. The trade-off is the higher average rate alongside fewer sold rooms, without asserting a proven cause or profit outcome.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Owen: Our average rate went up on the second night, so I have called it a complete commercial improvement. Is there anything missing from that summary?
  2. Mina: The higher ADR is real, but it is only one measure. We should look at how many rooms sold and what the available room supply earned as well.
  3. Owen: Start with the room supply. I do not want to mistake a capacity change for performance.
  4. Mina: Both nights had one hundred rooms available. Night A sold eighty rooms for eight thousand dollars. Night B sold sixty rooms for seven thousand two hundred dollars. These are room-only revenue figures.
  5. Owen: So the first average is one hundred dollars per sold room, and the second is one hundred and twenty. That is a twenty-percent increase.
  6. Mina: Correct. Meanwhile, occupancy moved from eighty percent to sixty percent. That is a fall of twenty percentage points, or twenty-five percent relative to the original eighty-percent level.
  7. Owen: I see the distinction. The higher rate applies to a smaller number of sold rooms. How should we describe the result across all available rooms?
  8. Mina: Use RevPAR. Eight thousand divided by one hundred is eighty dollars. Seven thousand two hundred divided by one hundred is seventy-two dollars. The capacity-based room revenue measure fell.
  9. Owen: That means an eight-dollar reduction per available room. Relative to eighty dollars, it is ten percent lower, despite the higher average selling rate.
  10. Mina: Yes. Total room revenue also fell ten percent, from eight thousand to seven thousand two hundred. The unchanged supply means those two percentage changes align in this comparison.
  11. Owen: Could we still say that profit improved because we had fewer rooms to service? Fewer occupied rooms might mean some costs were lower.
  12. Mina: That is possible, but we have no cost figures here. We cannot calculate profit or assume how expenses changed. A possible cost effect should remain a question, not become a reported financial result.
  13. Owen: Fair point. What about restaurant and event spending? Those could change the property's total revenue even when the room figures move in the opposite direction.
  14. Mina: They could, but non-room revenue is not included in this comparison. We should label the scope clearly instead of using the room result as a complete measure of hotel performance.
  15. Owen: Can I say the price increase caused the occupancy fall, or do we need to look at the business mix first?

  17. Owen: Then the next discussion should look at context rather than force a cause into the headline. Which breakdown would make that review more useful?

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Higher room price, lower room revenue

Fictional daily comparison: 100 available rooms on each day; day one sells 80 at an average of 100 units, day two sells 60 at 120 units. Figures exclude other revenue.

  1. Hotel manager: Average daily rate increased from 100 to 120. Surely room revenue improved as well.
  2. Revenue analyst: Not in this example. We sold 80 rooms before, but only 60 on the second day.
  3. Hotel manager: I understand occupancy fell, but how does that affect the measure on this slide?
  4. Revenue analyst: RevPAR is room revenue per available room. It includes the effect of unsold rooms.
  5. Hotel manager: So ADR describes the rooms sold, while RevPAR also reflects the available room inventory?
  6. Revenue analyst: Exactly. Room revenue was 8,000, then 7,200, across the same 100 available rooms.
  7. Hotel manager: That gives RevPAR of 80 and 72, despite the higher average selling price.
  8. Revenue analyst: Correct. Neither measure alone tells us profit, because we haven't included the relevant costs.
  9. Hotel manager: I'll explain the price increase and occupancy decline together in the management briefing.
  10. Revenue analyst: Good. Keep the comparison limited to these two days and these room-revenue figures.
A compression night is not every night

Separate fictional case: the hotel has high demand on Saturday during a convention, but Friday and Sunday bookings remain lower.

  1. Conference sales manager: You called Saturday a compression night. Does that mean the entire weekend is sold out?
  2. Revenue manager: No. Here it describes concentrated demand on Saturday; Friday and Sunday bookings are still lower.
  3. Conference sales manager: Then why does the proposed group quote show different prices across the three nights?
  4. Revenue manager: The proposal reflects those different demand conditions. Yield management considers when and how inventory is sold.
  5. Conference sales manager: Could I explain it as charging every convention guest more because they must attend?
  6. Revenue manager: That would misrepresent the proposal. Explain the specific nightly prices and conditions without guessing guests' motives.
  7. Conference sales manager: Would higher Saturday demand establish that the whole three-night package produces better revenue?
  8. Revenue manager: We'd still need the full booking mix and room revenue across all three nights.
  9. Conference sales manager: I'll describe Saturday separately and avoid saying the entire weekend has identical demand.
  10. Revenue manager: That distinction will help the planner compare the package without confusing a peak with the average.
Room revenue is not total event revenue

Separate fictional case: a conference booking includes 4,000 units of room revenue and 2,000 of banquet revenue; a trainee puts 6,000 into the hotel's room-revenue measure.

  1. Revenue controller: Your calculation includes banquet revenue in the room-revenue total. Can we separate those amounts?
  2. Management trainee: The conference brought in 6,000 overall, so I thought all of it belonged in RevPAR.
  3. Revenue controller: For this report, RevPAR uses room revenue. The banquet amount belongs in its own category.
  4. Management trainee: Then 4,000 is the room component, and 2,000 belongs to food and event service?
  5. Revenue controller: Correct. Combining them would make this booking look stronger against a room-only comparison unfairly.
  6. Management trainee: Does separating the figures mean the banquet business is less valuable to the hotel?
  7. Revenue controller: No. It means we're keeping the measures consistent before discussing the total business contribution.
  8. Management trainee: I'll show room revenue and banquet revenue separately, then give the combined total below.
  9. Revenue controller: Good. What additional information would you need before calling that total a profit figure?
  10. Management trainee: The associated costs. Revenue tells us what came in, not what remains after expenses.

Choose the right denominator

A hotel has 50 available rooms for one night. It sells 40 rooms and receives $6,000 in room revenue. No costs or non-room revenue are supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "Occupancy is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

B. 80

Forty sold rooms divided by fifty available rooms equals eighty percent.

2. Manager: "ADR is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. 150

Six thousand dollars divided by forty sold rooms equals one hundred fifty.

3. Analyst: "RevPAR is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 120

Six thousand dollars divided by fifty available rooms equals one hundred twenty.

4. Manager: "We cannot calculate ___ from these figures alone."

Answer and explanation

A. profit

The case supplies revenue but no relevant cost figures.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Revenue Management and Pricing
Original fictional case: Revenue analyst Mina reviews two single-night results with general manager Owen. Each night had 100 available rooms. On Night A, 80 rooms sold and room revenue was $8,000. On Night B, 60 rooms sold and room revenue was $7,200. These figures exclude non-room revenue; no cost figures are supplied. Owen calls the higher average daily rate a complete commercial improvement. Mina needs to distinguish the higher rate among sold rooms from the lower occupancy and lower revenue per available room. The two observations alone do not establish why demand changed.
Language workshop: A higher room rate did not produce higher room revenue
Communication goal: Explain occupancy, average daily rate, and revenue per available room using the correct denominators.
Language guidance: Night A: occupancy 80%, ADR $100, RevPAR $80. Night B: occupancy 60%, ADR $120, RevPAR $72. ADR increased 20%, but RevPAR and total room revenue each decreased 10%. Occupancy fell 20 percentage points.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which measure do we mean by improved? / Both nights had 100 available rooms. / Night A sold 80 rooms for $8,000. / Night B sold 60 rooms for $7,200. / Divide room revenue by rooms sold. / ADR rose from $100 to $120. / Sixty of one hundred rooms sold, or 60%. / Occupancy fell by 20 percentage points. / Divide room revenue by rooms available. / RevPAR fell from $80 to $72. / Room revenue decreased by $800, or 10%. / We do not have the costs needed to calculate profit. / These figures concern rooms, not all hotel revenue. / This comparison does not isolate the effect of pricing. / Let us examine demand, segments, and channel mix. / A higher sold-room rate coincided with fewer rooms sold.
Vocabulary: average daily rate (ADR): Room revenue divided by rooms sold for the period. / occupancy rate: Rooms sold as a percentage of rooms available. / revenue per available room (RevPAR): Room revenue divided by available room supply. / room revenue: Revenue from selling guest rooms. / rooms sold: The count of paid room units sold in the stated period. / rooms available: Room supply available for the stated reporting period. / room night: One room for one night. / non-room revenue: Revenue from services other than room sales. / revenue management: Coordinating price and availability to manage revenue. / rate strategy: The approach used to set and vary selling prices. / demand: Customer willingness and ability to purchase accommodation. / booking pace: The speed at which reservations accumulate for a date. / pickup: The change in booked business between reporting points. / on-the-books: Reservations already recorded for a future period. / forecast occupancy: The estimated share of rooms expected to sell. / market segment: A defined customer or business category. / channel mix: The proportions sold through different booking routes. / distribution cost: The expense associated with selling through a channel. / net room revenue: Room revenue after the specified deductions. / gross operating profit: Operating revenue less the relevant operating expenses. / competitive set: A selected group of properties used for comparison. / rate premium: A price above a stated comparison rate. / price elasticity: The responsiveness of demand to price changes. / revenue trade-off: A gain in one revenue driver offset by another change.
Speaking task: Read turns 3-10, stating whether each calculation uses rooms sold or rooms available. Repeat turns 11-16, separating revenue, profit, and a causal claim about pricing. Switch roles for the transfer and read 80%, $150, and $120 with their units.
Partner: Mina: Revenue analyst / Owen: General manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A hotel has 50 available rooms for one night. It sells 40 rooms and receives $6,000 in room revenue. No costs or non-room revenue are supplied.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Hospitality and Tourism English
Professional audience: hotel managers, front-desk teams, guest-relations staff, event coordinators, restaurant managers, tour operators, revenue managers, and destination-service professionals
Scope: All properties, people, figures, and policies in the cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace language, not legal advice or safety procedures. Lesson 04: Revenue Management and Pricing
Original fictional case: Revenue analyst Mina reviews two single-night results with general manager Owen. Each night had 100 available rooms. On Night A, 80 rooms sold and room revenue was $8,000. On Night B, 60 rooms sold and room revenue was $7,200. These figures exclude non-room revenue; no cost figures are supplied. Owen calls the higher average daily rate a complete commercial improvement. Mina needs to distinguish the higher rate among sold rooms from the lower occupancy and lower revenue per available room. The two observations alone do not establish why demand changed.
Language workshop: A higher room rate did not produce higher room revenue
Communication goal: Explain occupancy, average daily rate, and revenue per available room using the correct denominators.
Language guidance: Night A: occupancy 80%, ADR $100, RevPAR $80. Night B: occupancy 60%, ADR $120, RevPAR $72. ADR increased 20%, but RevPAR and total room revenue each decreased 10%. Occupancy fell 20 percentage points.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which measure do we mean by improved? / Both nights had 100 available rooms. / Night A sold 80 rooms for $8,000. / Night B sold 60 rooms for $7,200. / Divide room revenue by rooms sold. / ADR rose from $100 to $120. / Sixty of one hundred rooms sold, or 60%. / Occupancy fell by 20 percentage points. / Divide room revenue by rooms available. / RevPAR fell from $80 to $72. / Room revenue decreased by $800, or 10%. / We do not have the costs needed to calculate profit. / These figures concern rooms, not all hotel revenue. / This comparison does not isolate the effect of pricing. / Let us examine demand, segments, and channel mix. / A higher sold-room rate coincided with fewer rooms sold.
Vocabulary: average daily rate (ADR): Room revenue divided by rooms sold for the period. / occupancy rate: Rooms sold as a percentage of rooms available. / revenue per available room (RevPAR): Room revenue divided by available room supply. / room revenue: Revenue from selling guest rooms. / rooms sold: The count of paid room units sold in the stated period. / rooms available: Room supply available for the stated reporting period. / room night: One room for one night. / non-room revenue: Revenue from services other than room sales. / revenue management: Coordinating price and availability to manage revenue. / rate strategy: The approach used to set and vary selling prices. / demand: Customer willingness and ability to purchase accommodation. / booking pace: The speed at which reservations accumulate for a date. / pickup: The change in booked business between reporting points. / on-the-books: Reservations already recorded for a future period. / forecast occupancy: The estimated share of rooms expected to sell. / market segment: A defined customer or business category. / channel mix: The proportions sold through different booking routes. / distribution cost: The expense associated with selling through a channel. / net room revenue: Room revenue after the specified deductions. / gross operating profit: Operating revenue less the relevant operating expenses. / competitive set: A selected group of properties used for comparison. / rate premium: A price above a stated comparison rate. / price elasticity: The responsiveness of demand to price changes. / revenue trade-off: A gain in one revenue driver offset by another change.
Speaking task: Read turns 3-10, stating whether each calculation uses rooms sold or rooms available. Repeat turns 11-16, separating revenue, profit, and a causal claim about pricing. Switch roles for the transfer and read 80%, $150, and $120 with their units.
Partner: Mina: Revenue analyst / Owen: General manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A hotel has 50 available rooms for one night. It sells 40 rooms and receives $6,000 in room revenue. No costs or non-room revenue are supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: Then the next discussion should look at context rather than force a cause into the headline. Which breakdown would make that review more useful?
Mina: Examine market segments and channel mix, alongside demand and booking pace. Keep the same period and definitions, and include relevant costs before making a profitability claim.
Owen: I will revise the summary to say that ADR increased while occupancy, RevPAR, and room revenue declined. That is less sweeping and more informative.
Mina: Exactly. It describes the observed trade-off without treating one favorable metric as the whole result. We can then investigate why it happened and whether the strategy should change.
END REFERENCE
05Housekeeping, Maintenance, and TurnoverClean does not yet mean released+

Your goal

Read back separate department statuses and keep a room unavailable until the required release is recorded.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Clean does not yet mean released

Room 417 is cleaned and inspected by housekeeping, but its door-lock repair is still open in the maintenance record. The fictional property requires both housekeeping clearance and maintenance clearance before the duty manager releases a room to reception. Neither maintenance clearance nor the final release is recorded for 417. Reception wants to assign it to an arriving guest. Room 419 has both clearances and a recorded release; its room type and the guest-required features have been checked and match. Reception can assign 419 without changing 417 to available or promising when its repair will finish.

Leah: Reception supervisor / Marco: Rooms-division coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is missing for Room 417?

Answer and explanation

A. Maintenance clearance and final release

Housekeeping is complete, but the repair remains open and release is unrecorded.

2. Which room can reception assign on the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

A. Room 419

Room 419 is released and its required features match the guest's needs.

3. Who performs the final release in this fictional process?

Answer and explanation

B. The duty manager

The brief explicitly assigns final release to the duty manager after both clearances.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Housekeeping clearance confirms its own checks. It does not close a maintenance work order or supply the duty manager's release. For 417, keep all three statuses distinct rather than translating clean into available.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
room turnover
Preparing a room between guest stays.coordinate room turnover
housekeeping clearance
Confirmation that the required housekeeping checks are complete.record housekeeping clearance
maintenance clearance
Confirmation that the required maintenance conditions are satisfied.obtain maintenance clearance
work order
A recorded request or instruction for maintenance work.update a work order
open repair
A repair not yet recorded as completed and cleared.track an open repair
door-lock fault
A reported problem with a door's locking system.report a door-lock fault
inspection record
Documentation of a completed check and its result.verify the inspection record
release authorization
Permission to make the room available for assignment.verify release authorization
room status
The recorded operational condition of a room.reconcile room status
property management system (PMS)
Software managing room, reservation, and guest operations.update the PMS
out of order
A property-defined status taking a room out of use.confirm an out-of-order status
out of service
A property-defined temporary unavailability status.check the out-of-service code
vacant clean
A room-status label indicating an unoccupied, cleaned room.distinguish vacant clean
vacant dirty
A room-status label indicating cleaning is still required.report vacant dirty
occupied room
A room currently assigned for guest use.identify an occupied room
room discrepancy
A conflict between room records or observations.resolve a room discrepancy
engineering department
The hotel team responsible for technical maintenance.contact the engineering department
defect report
A record describing a fault needing attention.submit a defect report
readback
Repetition of details to check shared understanding.request a status readback
handover log
A record used to transfer operational responsibility.update the handover log
release hold
A restriction preventing a room from being assigned.maintain the release hold
guest-required feature
A room characteristic needed for the particular booking.verify guest-required features
return to service
Authorized restoration of availability after required checks.confirm return to service
status reconciliation
Checking and resolving differences among recorded statuses.complete status reconciliation

Codes such as out of order and out of service vary by property and reporting system. Confirm the local definition instead of assuming a universal meaning. In this case, the explicit release process governs assignment.

C Notice the language

Department labels answer different questions

Clean, repaired, inspected, and available may belong to separate checks. A colleague who insists on reading each status is protecting the handover, not criticizing another team. State the missing authorization and the ready alternative without blaming an entire department.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the exact room
I am checking the release status for Room 417.
Separate department checks
Housekeeping is cleared, but maintenance is not.
Identify the open item
The door-lock repair remains open.
State the missing authority
The duty manager's final release is not recorded.
Keep the hold clear
Do not assign 417 yet.
Avoid a shortcut
Vacant clean is not the same as released under this process.
Ask for evidence
Please check the clearance and release records.
Avoid an invented deadline
I do not have a confirmed repair completion time.
Offer the ready alternative
Room 419 has both clearances and final release.
Confirm suitability
Its room type and required features match.
Read back the action
Assign 419 and keep 417 unavailable.
Keep records consistent
Update the handover log without changing the repair status.
Use neutral language
The maintenance clearance is still pending.
Name the next owner
The duty manager can release it after the required clearances.
Report the guest outcome
We can accommodate the guest in the released alternative.
Close the handover
Please confirm that reception has the same room status.
6 language and precision notes
Cleaned versus cleared
A physical task and a recorded authorization may be different stages.
Still open
Describes the record status without assigning blame.
Not yet
Marks a pending condition without promising when it will change.
Released
Must refer to the relevant authorized decision.
Matches
Confirm actual required features, not only a room-category label.
Keep unavailable
Maintains the hold rather than predicting a technical outcome.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Assign 419; keep 417 on hold until its required clearances and release are recorded.

The released and suitable alternative meets the guest need without bypassing 417's outstanding requirements.

2. What does the housekeeping inspection prove here?

Answer and explanation

D. The housekeeping checks are complete.

A department-specific inspection does not establish another department's clearance or final authorization.

3. Which statement about room-status codes is safest?

Answer and explanation

D. Check the property's definitions and release rules.

Local status definitions and the stated release process determine their operational meaning.

4. Which response avoids blaming a team?

Answer and explanation

C. Maintenance clearance is pending; I will check the responsible contact.

It states the actionable status and next step without an unsupported general accusation.

Clean does not yet mean released

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leah: Reception supervisor / Marco: Rooms-division coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Leah
    Housekeeping says Room 417 is clean. The guest is waiting, so can I assign it now? The desk needs a clear answer before issuing keys.
  2. Marco
    Keep the 1 in place. Housekeeping has completed its inspection, but the door-lock repair remains open. We need the remaining clearance and the duty manager's release.
  3. Leah
    The room screen shows vacant clean. I assumed that meant it was available, since the cleaning team had already finished its part.
  4. Marco
    That label describes 2 here, not every release condition. We must check the separate maintenance and authorization records rather than infer that all teams have completed their work.
  5. Leah
    I see an open work order for the lock. Could it simply be an old entry that someone forgot to close after finishing the repair?
  6. Marco
    It could need checking, but the 3 is still open. We cannot turn that possibility into a completed repair. Ask the responsible team for the recorded status.
  7. Leah
    The guest is still at the desk. Is 419 released, and does it match the booking? I would rather offer a confirmed alternative now.
  8. Marco
    Room 419 has 4, housekeeping clearance, and final release. Its room type and the guest-required features have been checked and match. It is the confirmed alternative available now.
  9. Leah
    Good. I will assign 419. I will not change 417 to available simply because we have solved this guest's immediate accommodation problem.
  10. Marco
    Exactly. The 5 and the unresolved repair are separate matters. The guest can be accommodated while 417 remains unavailable under the current release process.
  11. Leah
    What should I say if the guest asks why the originally considered room is not ready? I do not want to share a speculative technical diagnosis.
  12. Marco
    Say that it is awaiting the required 6. Then explain that we have a released room matching the booking. Do not invent a repair deadline or blame a department.
  13. Leah
    I will put both statuses in the handover. The next shift needs to see why 417 says clean on one screen but unavailable on the other.
  14. Marco
    That calls for 7. The labels may describe different checks rather than contradictory facts. Confirm their definitions and ensure reception sees whether final release has actually occurred.
  15. Leah
    For the handover, I will record 419 as the assigned room and 417 as unavailable, with its lock work order still open pending verification.
  16. Marco
    Include that in the 8, together with the contact responsible for checking the maintenance status. Avoid marking the repair complete merely to make two screens display the same word.
  17. Leah
    Once the repair is cleared, does reception release 417 directly, or does the duty manager still need to record the final decision?
  18. Marco
    The duty manager records 9 after both clearances. That is the process in this property. A maintenance update alone does not remove the final authorization requirement.
  19. Leah
    Let me repeat the action: assign 419, keep 417 unavailable, check the maintenance record, and wait for the duty manager's release before assigning 417.
  20. Marco
    That 10 is correct. Please confirm the same status with the next shift. We have a practical guest solution and a separate outstanding room issue.

Answers and explanations
  1. release hold Turn 2. The release hold prevents assignment while the required maintenance clearance and final authorization remain outstanding.
  2. housekeeping clearance Turn 4. Housekeeping clearance covers housekeeping's checks and cannot stand in for maintenance clearance or the final room release.
  3. work order Turn 6. The work order is the maintenance record requiring verification; its possible staleness is not proof the repair is complete.
  4. maintenance clearance Turn 8. Maintenance clearance is already recorded for 419, alongside its housekeeping clearance and final release.
  5. room assignment Turn 10. The room assignment concerns 419 only; providing that alternative does not change the unresolved status of 417.
  6. release authorization Turn 12. Release authorization is the missing operational permission, which can be described without guessing at the technical fault or repair time.
  7. status reconciliation Turn 14. Status reconciliation checks what each label means and resolves the records without automatically treating different department statuses as contradictions.
  8. handover log Turn 16. The handover log transfers the actual room assignment, unresolved item, and responsibility to the next shift.
  9. return to service Turn 18. Return to service requires the stated final release after both department clearances; reception does not bypass that step.
  10. readback Turn 20. The readback confirms the room numbers, hold, and release sequence so the two departments act on the same information.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Cleaned is not released

Fictional shift handoff: room 214 is cleaned, but its maintenance repair request remains open and its assignment status has not been cleared.

  1. Housekeeping supervisor: Room 214 has been cleaned, but the maintenance request remains open on our handover list.
  2. Front desk lead: The cleaning update reached reception. Does that mean I can assign the room now?
  3. Housekeeping supervisor: No release is recorded. Cleaned describes our work, not confirmation that the repair is complete.
  4. Front desk lead: Who has accepted the follow-up? I don't want another shift assuming someone checked it.
  5. Housekeeping supervisor: I still own the follow-up and will contact maintenance for the outstanding repair status.
  6. Front desk lead: Please keep the room status clear while you do that; our arrival queue is growing.
  7. Housekeeping supervisor: Understood. I'll report the verified status to you rather than estimating when it becomes available.
  8. Front desk lead: So 214 is cleaned, repair unresolved, and you are checking with maintenance. Correct?
  9. Housekeeping supervisor: Correct. Any release must come through the hotel's authorized room-status process after that check.
  10. Front desk lead: I've accepted the reception handoff and will ensure the cleaning note isn't treated as release.
An out-of-order room carries into tomorrow

Separate fictional case: room 509 is out of order pending a replacement part; the supplier has not confirmed delivery. An incoming maintenance coordinator takes over supplier follow-up.

  1. Outgoing maintenance coordinator: Room 509 remains out of order. The replacement part is requested, but delivery isn't confirmed.
  2. Incoming maintenance coordinator: Has anyone told reception that tomorrow's turnover can include it in available inventory?
  3. Outgoing maintenance coordinator: No. Our last update said the delivery timing was unknown and the repair remained open.
  4. Incoming maintenance coordinator: I'll take supplier follow-up. Where is the part reference and the most recent contact recorded?
  5. Outgoing maintenance coordinator: Both are in the maintenance request for 509, with the last call marked unanswered.
  6. Incoming maintenance coordinator: An unanswered call isn't supplier acceptance. I'll keep that distinction in the next update.
  7. Outgoing maintenance coordinator: Thank you. Please also tell reception who now owns the follow-up on this room.
  8. Incoming maintenance coordinator: I'll confirm ownership to reception and report the supplier's response without promising a release date.
  9. Outgoing maintenance coordinator: Can you repeat the current status so I know the key information transferred correctly?
  10. Incoming maintenance coordinator: 509 stays out of order; delivery and repair are pending, and I own supplier follow-up.
A preventive visit clashes with a group arrival

Separate fictional case: preventive maintenance is booked for room 318 tomorrow morning, and a new group allocation lists the same room; no revised appointment is approved.

  1. Rooms coordinator: Tomorrow's group allocation includes 318, but preventive maintenance is still scheduled there that morning.
  2. Maintenance planner: Thanks for flagging it. Has the room allocation already been communicated as final to the group?
  3. Rooms coordinator: It's an internal draft. I haven't confirmed that room number to the tour coordinator.
  4. Maintenance planner: Then please keep the conflict visible. A proposed allocation doesn't cancel the maintenance appointment.
  5. Rooms coordinator: Can you take responsibility for checking whether the appointment can move without affecting the plan?
  6. Maintenance planner: Yes, I'll ask the authorized scheduler. I cannot approve that change myself during this handoff.
  7. Rooms coordinator: I'll retain ownership of the group allocation and wait for your scheduling update.
  8. Maintenance planner: To confirm, you manage room assignment, and I check the unresolved preventive maintenance conflict.
  9. Rooms coordinator: Exactly. Neither of us should mark the room available on the strength of this conversation.
  10. Maintenance planner: Agreed. I'll return a confirmed scheduling status, or clearly state that approval is still pending.
