Vocabulary: room turnover: Preparing a room between guest stays. / housekeeping clearance: Confirmation that the required housekeeping checks are complete. / maintenance clearance: Confirmation that the required maintenance conditions are satisfied. / work order: A recorded request or instruction for maintenance work. / open repair: A repair not yet recorded as completed and cleared. / door-lock fault: A reported problem with a door's locking system. / inspection record: Documentation of a completed check and its result. / release authorization: Permission to make the room available for assignment. / room status: The recorded operational condition of a room. / property management system (PMS): Software managing room, reservation, and guest operations. / out of order: A property-defined status taking a room out of use. / out of service: A property-defined temporary unavailability status. / vacant clean: A room-status label indicating an unoccupied, cleaned room. / vacant dirty: A room-status label indicating cleaning is still required. / occupied room: A room currently assigned for guest use. / room discrepancy: A conflict between room records or observations. / engineering department: The hotel team responsible for technical maintenance. / defect report: A record describing a fault needing attention. / readback: Repetition of details to check shared understanding. / handover log: A record used to transfer operational responsibility. / release hold: A restriction preventing a room from being assigned. / guest-required feature: A room characteristic needed for the particular booking. / return to service: Authorized restoration of availability after required checks. / status reconciliation: Checking and resolving differences among recorded statuses.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 05: Housekeeping, Maintenance, and Turnover
Original fictional case: Room 417 is cleaned and inspected by housekeeping, but its door-lock repair is still open in the maintenance record. The fictional property requires both housekeeping clearance and maintenance clearance before the duty manager releases a room to reception. Neither maintenance clearance nor the final release is recorded for 417. Reception wants to assign it to an arriving guest. Room 419 has both clearances and a recorded release; its room type and the guest-required features have been checked and match. Reception can assign 419 without changing 417 to available or promising when its repair will finish.
Language workshop: Clean does not yet mean released

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Events, Banquets, and Run of ShowThe soundcheck everyone assumed someone else had booked+

Your goal

Turn an assumed event arrangement into a timed, owned, and acknowledged schedule entry.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

The soundcheck everyone assumed someone else had booked

Venue coordinator Ren and event planner Asha compare their schedules. The soundcheck has no confirmed time, room, or owner; each team assumed the other arranged it. They now have a verified slot in Room A from 10:30 to 11:00. Audio lead Vera has accepted responsibility, the required equipment is available, and audience doors open at 11:15. Vera will check the event microphone and playback feed during the slot. Ren will update the controlled run of show and send it to the affected teams; Asha will confirm their acknowledgement. Later changes require coordinated review rather than a silent edit.

Ren: Venue coordinator / Asha: Event planner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was missing from the earlier soundcheck arrangement?

Answer and explanation

D. Confirmed time, room, and owner

Both teams assumed the other had arranged the task, leaving these details unconfirmed.

2. How long is the confirmed soundcheck slot?

Answer and explanation

D. Thirty minutes

The slot runs from 10:30 until 11:00, a thirty-minute duration.

3. What buffer remains before doors open?

Answer and explanation

D. Fifteen minutes

Soundcheck finishes at 11:00 and doors open at 11:15.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The soundcheck is 10:30-11:00 in Room A, owned by Vera. Doors open at 11:15, leaving a 15-minute buffer. Moving the soundcheck finish to 11:15 would consume that buffer even if the start remained unchanged.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
run of show
The timed sequence of event activities and responsibilities.update the run of show
soundcheck
A scheduled test of the event's audio setup.coordinate the soundcheck
audio lead
The person responsible for the audio work.confirm the audio lead
playback feed
The audio signal from recorded material into the system.test the playback feed
microphone check
Verification of the required microphone's operation.complete a microphone check
doors open
The time attendees may enter the event space.confirm doors-open time
cue
A signal to begin a particular event action.call the cue
cue sheet
A list of event signals and related actions.verify the cue sheet
stage manager
The person coordinating actions around the stage.brief the stage manager
technical rehearsal
A practice session checking technical event elements.schedule a technical rehearsal
room booking
A confirmed allocation of a space for a time.verify the room booking
setup window
Time allocated to preparing the event space.protect the setup window
strike
Removal of event equipment after use.schedule the strike
load-in
Bringing equipment into the event location.coordinate the load-in
load-out
Taking equipment out after the event.confirm the load-out
banquet event order (BEO)
A detailed document specifying an event's service requirements.reconcile the banquet event order
function sheet
A property document listing an event's operational requirements.check the function sheet
headcount
The number of people expected or present.confirm the headcount
room layout
The arrangement of seating, tables, and equipment.approve the room layout
buffer time
Time reserved between activities for transition or delay.preserve buffer time
task owner
The person accountable for completing a specified activity.name the task owner
schedule version
An identified edition of the event timetable.circulate the schedule version
acknowledgement
Confirmation that a message or instruction has been received.obtain team acknowledgement
change control
The process for reviewing and communicating alterations.follow event change control

Sending the updated run of show does not establish that every affected team has received and understood it. Record the version, secure acknowledgement, and coordinate changes that affect the audio lead, room, or doors-open handover.

C Notice the language

Shared awareness is not shared responsibility

An event team may all know that a soundcheck is needed while nobody has actually arranged it. Name the owner, location, start, finish, and affected handovers. Asking for a readback can be a practical coordination habit rather than a sign of distrust.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Expose the assumption
We both assumed the other team had arranged it.
Name the missing details
The schedule lacks a time, room, and owner.
Confirm the slot
Room A is available from 10:30 to 11:00.
Name the owner
Vera has accepted responsibility for the soundcheck.
Specify the scope
She will check the event microphone and playback feed.
Confirm readiness
The required equipment is available.
Protect the next event
Doors open at 11:15.
State the buffer
That leaves fifteen minutes between soundcheck and admission.
Update the shared record
I will revise the controlled run of show.
Identify the current version
Please use the updated schedule version.
Request acknowledgement
Confirm that the affected teams have received the change.
Avoid a silent edit
Any later change needs coordinated review.
Read back the details
Room A, 10:30 to 11:00, with Vera responsible.
Distinguish duration
The soundcheck lasts thirty minutes.
Clarify distribution
Ren will send the schedule; Asha will collect acknowledgements.
Close the gap
The task now has a confirmed time, place, and owner.
6 language and precision notes
Booked versus assumed
A shared expectation is not a verified arrangement.
From ... to
States the complete time interval, not only its start.
Doors open
Means attendee admission, not the soundcheck start.
Owner
Name a person with accepted responsibility, not merely a department.
Sent versus acknowledged
Delivery action does not establish receipt or understanding.
Buffer
A planned gap; do not silently allocate it to another activity.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which schedule entry is complete?

Answer and explanation

A. Soundcheck: Room A, 10:30-11:00, Vera, microphone and playback feed.

Only the complete entry preserves all confirmed details: location, interval, named owner, and test scope.

2. What happens if soundcheck ends at 11:15 with doors unchanged?

Answer and explanation

B. The fifteen-minute buffer disappears.

The revised finish coincides with admission, leaving no transition buffer.

3. Which statement correctly separates responsibilities?

Answer and explanation

D. Ren updates and sends; Asha confirms affected-team acknowledgement.

The brief assigns preparation and distribution to Ren and acknowledgement follow-up to Asha.

4. What is the best response to a later timing change?

Answer and explanation

D. Review its effects with the relevant teams and issue an acknowledged update.

Changes can affect dependencies and must reach the teams expected to act on them.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

Answers and explanations
  1. assumption Turn 2. The assumption is the unverified belief that another team had arranged the soundcheck; it left essential details unconfirmed.
  2. soundcheck Turn 4. The soundcheck is the scheduled audio test Vera will conduct in the verified room and time slot.
  3. task owner Turn 6. The task owner is the named person who has accepted responsibility, avoiding an ambiguous department-level assignment.
  4. buffer time Turn 8. Buffer time is the fifteen-minute interval between the eleven o'clock soundcheck finish and eleven-fifteen audience admission.
  5. playback feed Turn 10. The playback feed is explicitly included alongside the event microphone in Vera's soundcheck scope.
  6. run of show Turn 12. The run of show is the shared timed event record that should carry the current soundcheck details.
  7. acknowledgement Turn 14. Acknowledgement confirms that the update reached the relevant teams; sending it alone does not establish that shared awareness.
  8. change control Turn 16. Change control requires reviewing and communicating the effect of a later adjustment rather than silently changing a dependent schedule.
  9. Doors open Turn 18. Doors open identifies the audience-admission time, which is eleven fifteen rather than the eleven o'clock technical finish.
  10. readback Turn 20. The readback verifies the agreed location, duration, owner, scope, and dependent admission time before circulation.

  18. Ren: Exactly. Doors open remains eleven fifteen. Keep that separate from setup and testing times, even though all three activities occur in the same room sequence.
  19. Asha: To confirm: Room A, ten thirty to eleven, Vera responsible, microphone and playback checked, then a fifteen-minute buffer before audience admission.
  20. Ren: That readback captures it. I will send the revised run of show, and you will collect team acknowledgements. We now have an arrangement instead of a shared assumption.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

What does sound check include?

Fictional banquet planning call: the run of show lists sound check without a room, time, or owner; the organizer knows the presenter needs a wireless microphone.

  1. Banquet coordinator: The run of show lists sound check, but it doesn't identify a room or responsible contact.
  2. Event organizer: I assumed your venue arranged that automatically. Our presenter needs a wireless microphone for the talk.
  3. Banquet coordinator: Could you clarify whether sound check means testing that microphone or rehearsing the whole presentation?
  4. Event organizer: Only the microphone test is required. I haven't arranged a full rehearsal with the presenter.
  5. Banquet coordinator: Which event room should we use? I shouldn't infer that from the incomplete schedule entry.
  6. Event organizer: That still needs confirmation. Can we identify the missing decisions before choosing a test time?
  7. Banquet coordinator: Yes. We need the room, presenter availability, and the AV contact who accepts the task.
  8. Event organizer: I'll confirm the room and presenter availability; can you identify the appropriate venue contact?
  9. Banquet coordinator: I can request that assignment. I'll keep the AV requirement pending in the banquet event order.
  10. Event organizer: Then no sound check is confirmed yet. We'll agree the time once those details are available.
Doors open for whom?

Separate fictional event case: a schedule says doors open at 17:00; suppliers need access, and attendees arrive at 18:00. The meaning of the 17:00 entry is unclear.

  1. Venue operations manager: Your schedule says doors open at five. Does that refer to suppliers or invited attendees?
  2. Conference producer: Attendees arrive at six, but I can't tell whether the five o'clock entry means supplier access.
  3. Venue operations manager: That distinction affects staffing. Could you confirm who created the entry before we brief the team?
  4. Conference producer: I'll ask the production assistant. In the meantime, let's label attendee arrival separately at six.
  5. Venue operations manager: Agreed. Do suppliers need loading access only, or access to the event room as well?
  6. Conference producer: That hasn't been specified. I should collect their access requirements instead of assuming both.
  7. Venue operations manager: Please include the access point and receiving contact when you confirm the run of show.
  8. Conference producer: Would you like me to call once those points are verified, before circulating the revised schedule?
  9. Venue operations manager: Yes. We can then check that the banquet event order and staffing brief use the same wording.
  10. Conference producer: I'll confirm the five o'clock meaning and supplier requirements; six remains the verified attendee arrival time.
A guarantee is not an attendance forecast

Separate fictional planning case: a draft BEO lists 90 expected attendees and a separate guaranteed count of 80; the organizer asks what number catering should prepare against.

  1. Catering manager: The banquet event order shows 90 expected attendees and a guaranteed count of 80.
  2. Association planner: I thought those were two labels for the same number. Which entry are you asking about?
  3. Catering manager: I'm asking which count has been agreed for service planning under this event's arrangements.
  4. Association planner: The forecast is 90, but I haven't checked the agreement behind the guaranteed count.
  5. Catering manager: Then we should verify that before changing quantities or making any statement about attrition charges.
  6. Association planner: Could you clarify what document and section your team needs me to check first?
  7. Catering manager: Please check the event agreement's attendance and catering provisions, then confirm the current approved count.
  8. Association planner: I'll ask our authorized organizer to review them. Can the forecast remain clearly labeled meanwhile?
  9. Catering manager: Yes. Expected attendance and an agreed guarantee should stay separate in the BEO until clarified.
  10. Association planner: Understood. I'll return with the approved count rather than treating my attendance estimate as authorization.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The soundcheck everyone assumed someone else had booked
  1. Read turns 3-8, stressing the start, finish, and separate doors-open time.
  2. Repeat turns 11-18, distinguishing the current schedule from superseded copies.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Protect the handover interval

An event test runs from 09:20 to 09:50 in Hall B. Audio lead Jo owns it. Doors open at 10:00. The coordinator must circulate the updated schedule and receive acknowledgement.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Planner: "The test lasts ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

C. 30

The interval from 09:20 to 09:50 is thirty minutes.

2. Coordinator: "The buffer before doors is ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

D. 10

Ten minutes separate the 09:50 finish from 10:00 admission.

3. Planner: "The named task owner is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Jo

The brief explicitly assigns responsibility to audio lead Jo.

4. Coordinator: "After circulation, collect team ___."

Answer and explanation

D. acknowledgement

Sending the schedule alone does not confirm the affected teams received it.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Language workshop: The soundcheck everyone assumed someone else had booked
Communication goal: Turn an assumed event arrangement into a timed, owned, and acknowledged schedule entry.
Language guidance: The soundcheck is 10:30-11:00 in Room A, owned by Vera. Doors open at 11:15, leaving a 15-minute buffer. Moving the soundcheck finish to 11:15 would consume that buffer even if the start remained unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Tour Operations and Traveler SafetyA weather update is not permission to depart+

Your goal

Communicate an active tour hold, identify decision authority, and give an update without promising departure.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A weather update is not permission to depart

At 09:10, thunderstorms have triggered the tour operator's local departure hold for a coastal excursion. Guests are already inside the hotel's designated indoor waiting lounge, away from windows. Tour-desk agent Camila must keep the group there under the current plan; she cannot clear departure. The operator has not confirmed a route, restart, or cancellation. Operations lead Ben is responsible for the operational decision and will send a status update at 09:30. No refund or alternative excursion has been approved. Camila can promise to relay that update, including an unchanged hold, but cannot promise that the group will leave at 09:30.

Camila: Tour-desk agent / Mr Ellis: Excursion guest

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the current operational status?

Answer and explanation

C. Departure is on hold under the operator's plan.

The operator's hold is active; no restart, route, or cancellation is confirmed.

2. What should the group do now in this case?

Answer and explanation

B. Remain in the designated indoor lounge under the current plan.

The supplied local plan keeps the group in its designated indoor waiting location.

3. Who makes the operational decision?

Answer and explanation

D. Operations lead Ben

The brief assigns the decision to Ben and does not give Camila departure-clearance authority.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

09:30 is the operator's status-update time. It is not a confirmed departure, restart, route, or cancellation time. Camila relays the authorized status; she does not replace the operations lead's decision with her own forecast.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
excursion
A planned short trip or organized visitor activity.coordinate a coastal excursion
tour operator
The organization responsible for providing the tour.contact the tour operator
departure hold
A temporary restriction preventing the tour from leaving.maintain the departure hold
operational clearance
Authorized permission to proceed under the relevant process.await operational clearance
operations lead
The person responsible for the operational decision.consult the operations lead
weather advisory
Information warning of relevant weather conditions.monitor weather advisories
thunderstorm
A storm involving thunder and lightning.respond to a thunderstorm
lightning safety plan
A documented procedure for managing lightning-related activity risk.follow the lightning safety plan
designated shelter
A specifically identified protective location in the local plan.use the designated shelter
indoor waiting area
An internal location assigned for guests awaiting instructions.identify the indoor waiting area
route assessment
A review of the conditions affecting a proposed route.await the route assessment
itinerary
The planned sequence of trip activities and locations.revise the itinerary
departure clearance
Approval for the group or vehicle to leave.distinguish departure clearance
restart decision
A determination that a suspended activity may resume.communicate the restart decision
cancellation notice
A message confirming that a planned service will not operate.issue a cancellation notice
contingency arrangement
A prepared alternative if the original plan cannot proceed.verify contingency arrangements
rebooking
Moving a reservation to another service or time.check rebooking options
refund eligibility
Whether the applicable conditions permit money to be returned.verify refund eligibility
passenger manifest
A controlled list of people on the tour or transport.check the passenger manifest
group accountability
Knowing where the members of a group are.maintain group accountability
meeting point
The specified place where a group is to assemble.confirm the meeting point
communication chain
The route by which authorized information reaches others.follow the communication chain
all-clear
An authorized statement that a specified restriction can end.avoid an assumed all-clear
travel disruption
An interruption to planned transport or activities.explain the travel disruption

The active local instruction is to remain in the designated indoor lounge. Do not substitute personal experience, guest pressure, or a brief change in appearance for the operator's safety process. Financial options require separate confirmation.

C Notice the language

Reassurance must not weaken the instruction

Guests under time pressure may hear an update time as a departure time. State the current hold first, identify the decision-maker, and repeat what guests should do now. A friendly tone should not turn an active safety instruction into an optional suggestion.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the hold first
Departure is currently on hold.
Explain the present action
Please remain in the designated indoor lounge.
Name the responsible authority
Our operations lead is reviewing the conditions.
Avoid a route promise
No route has been confirmed.
Give the communication time
The operator will send an update at 09:30.
Separate the meanings
That is an update time, not a departure time.
Keep the instruction active
The hold remains in place until authorized instructions change.
Reject an informal all-clear
I cannot clear departure based on how the sky looks.
Acknowledge the inconvenience
I understand this affects your plans for the day.
Avoid guessing
I do not have a verified restart time.
Keep cancellation distinct
Cancellation has not been confirmed.
Keep money decisions distinct
No refund decision has been approved yet.
Own the communication
I will relay the operator's update to the group.
Preserve an unchanged status
I will update you even if the hold remains.
Clarify a conditional option
Any alternative excursion would need confirmation.
Close with the current instruction
For now, please stay in this designated indoor area.
6 language and precision notes
On hold
A temporary operational status, not automatically a cancellation.
At 09:30
Attach the time to update so it is not heard as departure.
Until
States the condition ending an instruction, not an estimated duration.
Not confirmed
Neither approved nor necessarily ruled out.
All-clear
Must come from the authorized process, not casual observation.
Will relay
Commits to communication rather than a favorable operational result.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which announcement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Departure remains on hold; the next operator update is at 09:30.

The announcement preserves the active hold and correctly labels the scheduled communication.

2. What should Camila do if guests demand an immediate departure?

Answer and explanation

A. Repeat the active instruction and refer the decision through the operator's process.

Guest pressure does not give the agent authority to override the active operational hold.

3. Which statement about cancellation is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. It has not been confirmed.

The brief distinguishes the current hold from an undecided cancellation.

4. Which financial statement fits the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Refund eligibility and any remedy still require confirmation.

No refund decision or alternative arrangement has been approved in the case.

E Conversations

A weather update is not permission to depart

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Camila: Tour-desk agent / Mr Ellis: Excursion guest

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • communication chain
  • route assessment
  • indoor waiting area
  • departure hold
  • rebooking
  • travel disruption
  • refund eligibility
  • status update
  • operational clearance
  • all-clear
  1. Mr Ellis
    Somebody mentioned nine thirty. Is that our new departure time? Should we get our bags ready outside?
  2. Camila
    No. The 1 remains in place. Please stay in this designated indoor lounge, away from the windows, under the operator's current plan. Nine thirty is the next update.
  3. Mr Ellis
    Thank you for making that clear. I am worried about losing the whole day, especially because we booked another activity later this afternoon.
  4. Camila
    I understand the 2. I will give you verified information as it arrives. At present, no route or restart time has been confirmed, so I cannot promise when the excursion will begin.
  5. Mr Ellis
    The sky looks brighter. Could the driver take us out now and avoid the worst part of the coast?
  6. Camila
    We need 3, not a judgment based on a brighter patch of sky. Our operations lead, Ben, is responsible for the decision. I cannot clear departure myself.
  7. Mr Ellis
    Has the operator canceled it, or are they still deciding? My family heard both versions in the lobby.
  8. Camila
    Correct. The 4 has not produced a confirmed route for us. Cancellation is also unconfirmed. The current status is a hold, with the group remaining in the designated indoor area.
  9. Mr Ellis
    Will we hear from you at nine thirty even without a decision, or should we keep asking at the desk?
  10. Camila
    I will relay the 5 to the group even if the hold is unchanged. You should not have to interpret silence or assume that no message means permission to leave.
  11. Mr Ellis
    I appreciate that. Would a cancellation mean that we automatically receive our money back, or move to another excursion today?
  12. Camila
    I need to verify 6 and any available alternatives before promising either outcome. No refund or replacement excursion has been approved. Those arrangements are separate from the immediate departure decision.
  13. Mr Ellis
    Please check with us before moving the booking. An afternoon replacement might clash with our other activity, even if it sounds similar.
  14. Camila
    Of course. Any 7 would need a confirmed option and your agreement. I will explain the actual itinerary and relevant terms if an alternative becomes available, rather than treating it as already arranged.
  15. Mr Ellis
    Where should our group wait so that we do not miss the announcement?
  16. Camila
    Stay together in this 8. If someone needs assistance, please tell the desk. We will use the operator's communication process and keep the current instruction clear until it changes.
  17. Mr Ellis
    I will tell the others that nine thirty means news, not departure. I will also explain that neither cancellation nor a refund has been decided.
  18. Camila
    That is accurate. There is no 9 at present. I know waiting is inconvenient, but I should not soften that message into a suggestion that the group may choose to depart.
  19. Mr Ellis
    Understood. We will remain here and wait for the operator's message. Please include us even if the next announcement only says the hold continues.
  20. Camila
    I will. The 10 is Ben to the desk and then to the group. I will make the current status and next instruction explicit when I relay the update.

Answers and explanations
  1. departure hold Turn 2. The departure hold is the active restriction on leaving, so guests should not interpret the update time as permission to board.
  2. travel disruption Turn 4. Travel disruption describes the effect on the guest's plans without implying that a restart or remedy is already confirmed.
  3. operational clearance Turn 6. Operational clearance must come through the authorized operator process; a change in appearance or an improvised route does not provide it.
  4. route assessment Turn 8. The route assessment concerns whether and how the excursion can operate; no route outcome has yet been confirmed.
  5. status update Turn 10. The status update must be passed on even if it only confirms that the existing hold continues.
  6. refund eligibility Turn 12. Refund eligibility depends on the applicable conditions and decision; the case does not establish an approved refund.
  7. rebooking Turn 14. Rebooking changes the reservation and would require a confirmed option and the guest's agreement, not an automatic assumption.
  8. indoor waiting area Turn 16. The designated indoor waiting area is the location specified by the current local plan, keeping guests available for the authorized update.
  9. all-clear Turn 18. An all-clear would require authorized instructions ending the restriction; none has been issued merely because an update is scheduled.
  10. communication chain Turn 20. The communication chain carries the operations lead's authorized status through the tour desk to the guests without inventing an intermediate decision.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr Ellis: Somebody mentioned nine thirty. Is that our new departure time? Should we get our bags ready outside?
  2. Camila: No. The departure hold remains in place. Please stay in this designated indoor lounge, away from the windows, under the operator's current plan. Nine thirty is the next update.
  3. Mr Ellis: Thank you for making that clear. I am worried about losing the whole day, especially because we booked another activity later this afternoon.
  4. Camila: I understand the travel disruption. I will give you verified information as it arrives. At present, no route or restart time has been confirmed, so I cannot promise when the excursion will begin.
  5. Mr Ellis: The sky looks brighter. Could the driver take us out now and avoid the worst part of the coast?
  6. Camila: We need operational clearance, not a judgment based on a brighter patch of sky. Our operations lead, Ben, is responsible for the decision. I cannot clear departure myself.
  7. Mr Ellis: Has the operator canceled it, or are they still deciding? My family heard both versions in the lobby.
  8. Camila: Correct. The route assessment has not produced a confirmed route for us. Cancellation is also unconfirmed. The current status is a hold, with the group remaining in the designated indoor area.
  9. Mr Ellis: Will we hear from you at nine thirty even without a decision, or should we keep asking at the desk?
  10. Camila: I will relay the status update to the group even if the hold is unchanged. You should not have to interpret silence or assume that no message means permission to leave.
  11. Mr Ellis: I appreciate that. Would a cancellation mean that we automatically receive our money back, or move to another excursion today?
  12. Camila: I need to verify refund eligibility and any available alternatives before promising either outcome. No refund or replacement excursion has been approved. Those arrangements are separate from the immediate departure decision.
  13. Mr Ellis: Please check with us before moving the booking. An afternoon replacement might clash with our other activity, even if it sounds similar.
  14. Camila: Of course. Any rebooking would need a confirmed option and your agreement. I will explain the actual itinerary and relevant terms if an alternative becomes available, rather than treating it as already arranged.
  15. Mr Ellis: Where should our group wait so that we do not miss the announcement?
  16. Camila: Stay together in this indoor waiting area. If someone needs assistance, please tell the desk. We will use the operator's communication process and keep the current instruction clear until it changes.
  17. Mr Ellis: I will tell the others that nine thirty means news, not departure. I will also explain that neither cancellation nor a refund has been decided.
  18. Camila: That is accurate. There is no all-clear at present. I know waiting is inconvenient, but I should not soften that message into a suggestion that the group may choose to depart.
  19. Mr Ellis: Understood. We will remain here and wait for the operator's message. Please include us even if the next announcement only says the hold continues.
  20. Camila: I will. The communication chain is Ben to the desk and then to the group. I will make the current status and next instruction explicit when I relay the update.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Weather concern without a departure decision

Fictional excursion coordination: heavy weather may affect a trip; the local operator has not confirmed whether it will proceed.

  1. Hotel concierge: Guests are asking whether tomorrow's excursion will follow the original itinerary despite the weather.
  2. Tour operations coordinator: Heavy weather may affect it, but the local operator hasn't confirmed a departure decision yet.
  3. Hotel concierge: The booking remains visible online. Can I treat that as confirmation the trip is proceeding?
  4. Tour operations coordinator: No. A visible booking isn't an operational update, and we shouldn't make that inference.
  5. Hotel concierge: What can I tell the group without making the situation sound more certain than it is?
  6. Tour operations coordinator: Say the itinerary is under review and we're awaiting the operator's confirmed status.
  7. Hotel concierge: Should I also say their waiver means the excursion is safe to run in this weather?
  8. Tour operations coordinator: No. A waiver doesn't establish current conditions or replace the operator's authorized safety decision.
  9. Hotel concierge: I'll keep the departure status unconfirmed and direct operational questions through your contact.
  10. Tour operations coordinator: I'll request the verified update and separate any itinerary change from unanswered refund questions.
A photograph is not a trail assessment

Separate fictional tour case: a guide receives an old photograph of a wet trail; no current route assessment has been provided by the local operator.

  1. Tour guide: A traveler shared a wet-trail photograph and says it proves tomorrow's route is closed.
  2. Excursion manager: Do we know when the photograph was taken or whether it shows our planned route?
  3. Tour guide: It's from last month, and the location hasn't been verified against the itinerary.
  4. Excursion manager: Then it doesn't establish today's route status. We need the local operator's current assessment.
  5. Tour guide: Can I dismiss the concern because the photograph is old and might show somewhere else?
  6. Excursion manager: Don't dismiss it. Acknowledge the concern while explaining why this evidence doesn't settle the question.
  7. Tour guide: I'll say we haven't confirmed a closure or established that the route is suitable.
  8. Excursion manager: That's accurate. Ask the operator for the current route status through our coordination channel.
  9. Tour guide: Until that arrives, I'll avoid proposing an alternative trail based on my own assumptions.
  10. Excursion manager: Good. Any revised itinerary needs the appropriate review, not just a reassuring answer to the group.
Cancellation does not settle the contract question

Separate fictional case: a local operator has confirmed cancellation of a harbor tour due to weather; the hotel's coordinator has not reviewed the booking's refund terms.

  1. Travel coordinator: The harbor tour is canceled because of weather. Does force majeure mean nobody receives a refund?
  2. Excursion supervisor: We have a confirmed cancellation, but we haven't reviewed the relevant terms for these bookings.
  3. Travel coordinator: The operator used that phrase in its message. I assumed it settled the payment question.
  4. Excursion supervisor: It doesn't let us infer each traveler's contractual position from this message alone.
  5. Travel coordinator: So the cancellation is known, while the refund outcome still needs an authorized review?
  6. Excursion supervisor: Exactly. We can explain the confirmed itinerary change and refer the payment question for review.
  7. Travel coordinator: Guests will want a definite answer. Can we at least promise a replacement tour?
  8. Excursion supervisor: No replacement is confirmed. We can ask about options without presenting them as approved compensation.
  9. Travel coordinator: I'll separate the operational announcement from the unresolved refund and alternative-tour questions.
  10. Excursion supervisor: Please do. I'll gather the booking terms and operator notice for the appropriate reviewer.

Lesson 07: Tour Operations and Traveler Safety
Original fictional case: At 09:10, thunderstorms have triggered the tour operator's local departure hold for a coastal excursion. Guests are already inside the hotel's designated indoor waiting lounge, away from windows. Tour-desk agent Camila must keep the group there under the current plan; she cannot clear departure. The operator has not confirmed a route, restart, or cancellation. Operations lead Ben is responsible for the operational decision and will send a status update at 09:30. No refund or alternative excursion has been approved. Camila can promise to relay that update, including an unchanged hold, but cannot promise that the group will leave at 09:30.
Language workshop: A weather update is not permission to depart
Communication goal: Communicate an active tour hold, identify decision authority, and give an update without promising departure.
Language guidance: 09:30 is the operator's status-update time. It is not a confirmed departure, restart, route, or cancellation time. Camila relays the authorized status; she does not replace the operations lead's decision with her own forecast.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Departure is currently on hold. / Please remain in the designated indoor lounge. / Our operations lead is reviewing the conditions. / No route has been confirmed. / The operator will send an update at 09:30. / That is an update time, not a departure time. / The hold remains in place until authorized instructions change. / I cannot clear departure based on how the sky looks. / I understand this affects your plans for the day. / I do not have a verified restart time. / Cancellation has not been confirmed. / No refund decision has been approved yet. / I will relay the operator's update to the group. / I will update you even if the hold remains. / Any alternative excursion would need confirmation. / For now, please stay in this designated indoor area.
Vocabulary: excursion: A planned short trip or organized visitor activity. / tour operator: The organization responsible for providing the tour. / departure hold: A temporary restriction preventing the tour from leaving. / operational clearance: Authorized permission to proceed under the relevant process. / operations lead: The person responsible for the operational decision. / weather advisory: Information warning of relevant weather conditions. / thunderstorm: A storm involving thunder and lightning. / lightning safety plan: A documented procedure for managing lightning-related activity risk. / designated shelter: A specifically identified protective location in the local plan. / indoor waiting area: An internal location assigned for guests awaiting instructions. / route assessment: A review of the conditions affecting a proposed route. / itinerary: The planned sequence of trip activities and locations. / departure clearance: Approval for the group or vehicle to leave. / restart decision: A determination that a suspended activity may resume. / cancellation notice: A message confirming that a planned service will not operate. / contingency arrangement: A prepared alternative if the original plan cannot proceed. / rebooking: Moving a reservation to another service or time. / refund eligibility: Whether the applicable conditions permit money to be returned. / passenger manifest: A controlled list of people on the tour or transport. / group accountability: Knowing where the members of a group are. / meeting point: The specified place where a group is to assemble. / communication chain: The route by which authorized information reaches others. / all-clear: An authorized statement that a specified restriction can end. / travel disruption: An interruption to planned transport or activities.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-6, putting the current waiting instruction before the explanation. Repeat turns 9-14, making update, departure, cancellation, and rebooking distinct. Switch roles for the transfer and read the unchanged hold without suggesting permission to board.
Partner: Camila: Tour-desk agent / Mr Ellis: Excursion guest
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A local tour hold remains active. The group is in the designated indoor lounge. The operator promises an 11:00 update but has not cleared a route, departure, cancellation, or refund.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr Ellis: I will tell the others that nine thirty means news, not departure. I will also explain that neither cancellation nor a refund has been decided.
Camila: That is accurate. There is no all-clear at present. I know waiting is inconvenient, but I should not soften that message into a suggestion that the group may choose to depart.
Mr Ellis: Understood. We will remain here and wait for the operator's message. Please include us even if the next announcement only says the hold continues.
Camila: I will. The communication chain is Ben to the desk and then to the group. I will make the current status and next instruction explicit when I relay the update.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

REFERENCE
Course: Hospitality and Tourism English
Professional audience: hotel managers, front-desk teams, guest-relations staff, event coordinators, restaurant managers, tour operators, revenue managers, and destination-service professionals
Scope: All properties, people, figures, and policies in the cases are fictional. This book teaches workplace language, not legal advice or safety procedures. Follow current local rules, approved service policies, privacy requirements, and operator emergency plans. Do not treat an exercise as operational authorization.
Lesson 07: Tour Operations and Traveler Safety
Original fictional case: At 09:10, thunderstorms have triggered the tour operator's local departure hold for a coastal excursion. Guests are already inside the hotel's designated indoor waiting lounge, away from windows. Tour-desk agent Camila must keep the group there under the current plan; she cannot clear departure. The operator has not confirmed a route, restart, or cancellation. Operations lead Ben is responsible for the operational decision and will send a status update at 09:30. No refund or alternative excursion has been approved. Camila can promise to relay that update, including an unchanged hold, but cannot promise that the group will leave at 09:30.
Language workshop: A weather update is not permission to depart
Communication goal: Communicate an active tour hold, identify decision authority, and give an update without promising departure.
Language guidance: 09:30 is the operator's status-update time. It is not a confirmed departure, restart, route, or cancellation time. Camila relays the authorized status; she does not replace the operations lead's decision with her own forecast.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Departure is currently on hold. / Please remain in the designated indoor lounge. / Our operations lead is reviewing the conditions. / No route has been confirmed. / The operator will send an update at 09:30. / That is an update time, not a departure time. / The hold remains in place until authorized instructions change. / I cannot clear departure based on how the sky looks. / I understand this affects your plans for the day. / I do not have a verified restart time. / Cancellation has not been confirmed. / No refund decision has been approved yet. / I will relay the operator's update to the group. / I will update you even if the hold remains. / Any alternative excursion would need confirmation. / For now, please stay in this designated indoor area.
Vocabulary: excursion: A planned short trip or organized visitor activity. / tour operator: The organization responsible for providing the tour. / departure hold: A temporary restriction preventing the tour from leaving. / operational clearance: Authorized permission to proceed under the relevant process. / operations lead: The person responsible for the operational decision. / weather advisory: Information warning of relevant weather conditions. / thunderstorm: A storm involving thunder and lightning. / lightning safety plan: A documented procedure for managing lightning-related activity risk. / designated shelter: A specifically identified protective location in the local plan. / indoor waiting area: An internal location assigned for guests awaiting instructions. / route assessment: A review of the conditions affecting a proposed route. / itinerary: The planned sequence of trip activities and locations. / departure clearance: Approval for the group or vehicle to leave. / restart decision: A determination that a suspended activity may resume. / cancellation notice: A message confirming that a planned service will not operate. / contingency arrangement: A prepared alternative if the original plan cannot proceed. / rebooking: Moving a reservation to another service or time. / refund eligibility: Whether the applicable conditions permit money to be returned. / passenger manifest: A controlled list of people on the tour or transport. / group accountability: Knowing where the members of a group are. / meeting point: The specified place where a group is to assemble. / communication chain: The route by which authorized information reaches others. / all-clear: An authorized statement that a specified restriction can end. / travel disruption: An interruption to planned transport or activities.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-6, putting the current waiting instruction before the explanation. Repeat turns 9-14, making update, departure, cancellation, and rebooking distinct. Switch roles for the transfer and read the unchanged hold without suggesting permission to board.
Partner: Camila: Tour-desk agent / Mr Ellis: Excursion guest
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A local tour hold remains active. The group is in the designated indoor lounge. The operator promises an 11:00 update but has not cleared a route, departure, cancellation, or refund.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr Ellis: I will tell the others that nine thirty means news, not departure. I will also explain that neither cancellation nor a refund has been decided.
Camila: That is accurate. There is no all-clear at present. I know waiting is inconvenient, but I should not soften that message into a suggestion that the group may choose to depart.
Mr Ellis: Understood. We will remain here and wait for the operator's message. Please include us even if the next announcement only says the hold continues.
Camila: I will. The communication chain is Ben to the desk and then to the group. I will make the current status and next instruction explicit when I relay the update.
END REFERENCE
08Cultural Expectations and Service StyleA timing preference is not a nationality-based rule+

Your goal

Clarify an individual service preference, limit its scope, and distinguish a request from a confirmed arrangement.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

A timing preference is not a nationality-based rule

Ms Rahman declines morning housekeeping on the first day of a three-night stay. A colleague assumes guests from her country never want housekeeping and proposes canceling routine service for the whole stay. Guest-relations agent Nico checks directly. Ms Rahman wants no routine room entry before 14:00 today and would like service after 14:00 if available. The afternoon team has not yet confirmed a slot. Her preference for later days has not been stated. Nico can record today's request and seek confirmation, but cannot promise the time or infer future preferences. Emergency access follows the property's separate rules.

Nico: Guest-relations agent / Ms Rahman: Staying guest

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Ms Rahman want before 14:00 today?

Answer and explanation

A. No routine room entry

Her stated preference concerns routine entry before a specific time today.

2. What is the status of afternoon service?

Answer and explanation

D. Requested but not yet confirmed

The afternoon team has not confirmed a slot, so the request remains pending.

3. What is known about later days?

Answer and explanation

C. No preference has been stated.

The brief explicitly limits the stated preference to today, not the entire stay.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

No routine entry before 14:00 today is narrower than no housekeeping for three nights. A request for service after 14:00 does not establish a start at exactly 14:00, nor guarantee that an afternoon slot exists.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
service preference
An individual's stated choice about how service is provided.clarify a service preference
routine room entry
Entry for ordinary scheduled room service tasks.limit routine room entry
do-not-disturb request
A request to avoid routine interruption.record a do-not-disturb request
stayover service
Housekeeping provided while a guest continues a stay.coordinate stayover service
service window
A period during which a service may be provided.confirm the service window
afternoon slot
An available time allocation later in the day.verify an afternoon slot
service frequency
How often a service is provided.clarify service frequency
opt out
Choose not to receive a specified optional service.opt out for today
opt in
Choose to receive a specified optional service.opt in to service
preference note
A record of an explicitly stated service choice.update the preference note
scope of request
The limits of what a person has asked for.confirm the scope of request
duration of stay
The total period of the guest's visit.distinguish the duration of stay
assumed preference
A choice attributed to someone without confirmation.question an assumed preference
stereotype
A generalized belief applied to an individual because of group membership.avoid a cultural stereotype
individual variation
Differences among people within the same group.recognize individual variation
clarifying question
A question resolving a specific ambiguity.ask a clarifying question
neutral wording
Language that avoids unsupported judgments or assumptions.use neutral wording
form of address
The name or title used when speaking to someone.ask the preferred form of address
language preference
The language a person chooses for communication.check language preference
communication style
A person's preferred manner of interaction.adapt communication style
personal space
The distance or privacy a person prefers in an interaction.respect personal space
interruption
An action that breaks someone's current activity or privacy.avoid an unnecessary interruption
service confirmation
Verification that the requested service has been arranged.await service confirmation
emergency access
Entry governed by separate urgent-situation procedures.distinguish emergency access

Record what the guest actually says, with the relevant day and service scope. Do not add nationality-based explanations or silently extend the preference to future days. Routine privacy requests do not redefine the property's separate emergency-access rules.

C Notice the language

Ask without making the guest defend a group

People differ within every nationality and language community. A neutral question about today's service is more useful than an explanation of what people from a country supposedly prefer. Confirm the individual choice, its time period, and whether the requested service is actually available.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about the service
Would you prefer service later today or no service today?
Confirm the time boundary
You would like no routine entry before 14:00 today.
Clarify the requested option
You would like housekeeping after 14:00 if available.
Keep availability honest
I still need to check an afternoon slot.
Avoid an exact-time promise
After 14:00 does not mean a confirmed start at 14:00.
Limit the period
I will record that for today only.
Avoid a group assumption
I will check your preference directly.
Use the guest's words
The note should describe the service and timing you requested.
Check a future preference
We have not yet discussed the later days.
Separate request and confirmation
The request is recorded; the service time is not confirmed.
Offer a clear next step
I will contact the afternoon team and confirm the available option.
Respect the interaction
I do not need a personal explanation for that preference.
Correct the earlier inference
Declining this morning did not mean declining the whole stay.
Keep the record neutral
No routine entry before 14:00 today; later service requested.
Bound the policy
Emergency access follows the property's separate rules.
Close with a readback
Let me repeat the request so I record it accurately.
6 language and precision notes
Today
Prevents a one-day preference from becoming a whole-stay rule.
After versus at
After sets a lower time boundary; at names a particular time.
If available
Keeps the requested option conditional until checked.
Decline
Specify exactly which service or timing was declined.
Would you prefer
Gives a neutral choice without demanding a cultural explanation.
Recorded versus arranged
A saved preference is not proof that staffing is confirmed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which note is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. No routine entry before 14:00 today; later service requested, slot pending.

The correct note preserves the guest's stated scope and the unconfirmed afternoon availability.

2. Which question avoids stereotyping?

Answer and explanation

B. Would you prefer service later today or no service today?

The neutral question asks about the individual's service choice rather than a presumed group trait.

3. What needs confirmation before promising afternoon service?

Answer and explanation

A. An available slot from the afternoon team

The requested time depends on staffing availability that has not yet been checked.

4. Which statement about future days is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Their service preference remains unstated.

The guest has only specified today's preference, so later days cannot be inferred.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

A timing preference is not a nationality-based rule

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nico: Guest-relations agent / Ms Rahman: Staying guest

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • factual
  • routine service
  • housekeeping note
  • whole stay
  • assumption
  • privacy
  • time window
  • preference
  • confirmation
  • afternoon slot
  1. Nico
    Ms Rahman, may I check your housekeeping request? Would you like service later today, or would you prefer to skip it today?
  2. Ms Rahman
    Later, please. I would like some 1 this morning. Please do not send anyone in before two, but I would still like the room serviced afterward if possible.
  3. Nico
    Certainly. I will ask the afternoon team what they can arrange, then come back to you with an available time.
  4. Ms Rahman
    Thank you. An 2 would work. It does not have to be exactly two. I just do not want another knock before then.
  5. Nico
    I also need to correct our earlier note. It says you declined housekeeping for all three nights. Is that what you intended?
  6. Ms Rahman
    No, not for the 3. I only declined this morning. Please do not cancel the other days because of that.
  7. Nico
    I am sorry; that note went beyond what you asked. I will correct it to today's request only.
  8. Ms Rahman
    Someone also said guests from my country usually want no service. That felt like an 4 about me. Please just ask what I need.
  9. Nico
    You are right. We should have asked you directly. I will keep the note about the service and timing, not your nationality.
  10. Ms Rahman
    Good. My 5 today is a quiet morning and housekeeping later if you have space. That is all I need the team to know.
  11. Nico
    I have entered no routine entry before two today; later housekeeping requested, awaiting the team's reply. Does that describe it correctly?
  12. Ms Rahman
    Yes. Could you make sure the 6 still says I want service later? I do not want the afternoon team to think I refused everything.
  13. Nico
    It does. This covers ordinary housekeeping visits; the hotel's separate emergency procedures are unchanged.
  14. Ms Rahman
    Understood. I am asking about 7, not emergencies. Please let me know if the afternoon team cannot fit me in.
  15. Nico
    I will. I have recorded the request, but I still need their answer before I can say it is arranged.
  16. Ms Rahman
    Please send me the 8 once you have it. A recorded request has been mistaken for a booking on another stay.
  17. Nico
    Certainly. We can check tomorrow's arrangements separately when you know what you would like.
  18. Ms Rahman
    That suits me. I may want a different 9 tomorrow, but I have not decided. Please leave tomorrow's timing open for now.
  19. Nico
    To confirm today's request: no routine visit before two, housekeeping afterward if available, and no change requested for the remaining days.
  20. Ms Rahman
    Correct. That is a clear, 10 note. Thank you for correcting it. I will wait for your message about the afternoon.

Answers and explanations
  1. privacy Turn 2. Privacy concerns the guest's request not to be interrupted before two today, not a whole-stay cancellation.
  2. afternoon slot Turn 4. An afternoon slot is the requested later housekeeping allocation; the team has not yet confirmed it.
  3. whole stay Turn 6. The whole stay covers all three nights, whereas the guest has only stated today's timing preference.
  4. assumption Turn 8. An assumption substitutes an unverified group-based belief for this particular guest's stated request.
  5. preference Turn 10. The preference is the individual's choice for today, not a permanent choice determined by nationality.
  6. housekeeping note Turn 12. The housekeeping note carries the practical request to the team and must not imply cancellation of all service.
  7. routine service Turn 14. Routine service means ordinary housekeeping, distinct from entry governed by a separate urgent-situation procedure.
  8. confirmation Turn 16. Confirmation establishes the arrangement after availability is checked; entering a request does not provide it.
  9. time window Turn 18. The time window is the period for service; tomorrow's period has not been requested or agreed.
  10. factual Turn 20. Factual describes a note limited to the stated service request, without cultural explanations or invented future preferences.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nico: Ms Rahman, may I check your housekeeping request? Would you like service later today, or would you prefer to skip it today?
  2. Ms Rahman: Later, please. I would like some privacy this morning. Please do not send anyone in before two, but I would still like the room serviced afterward if possible.
  3. Nico: Certainly. I will ask the afternoon team what they can arrange, then come back to you with an available time.
  4. Ms Rahman: Thank you. An afternoon slot would work. It does not have to be exactly two. I just do not want another knock before then.
  5. Nico: I also need to correct our earlier note. It says you declined housekeeping for all three nights. Is that what you intended?
  6. Ms Rahman: No, not for the whole stay. I only declined this morning. Please do not cancel the other days because of that.
  7. Nico: I am sorry; that note went beyond what you asked. I will correct it to today's request only.
  8. Ms Rahman: Someone also said guests from my country usually want no service. That felt like an assumption about me. Please just ask what I need.
  9. Nico: You are right. We should have asked you directly. I will keep the note about the service and timing, not your nationality.
  10. Ms Rahman: Good. My preference today is a quiet morning and housekeeping later if you have space. That is all I need the team to know.
  11. Nico: I have entered no routine entry before two today; later housekeeping requested, awaiting the team's reply. Does that describe it correctly?
  12. Ms Rahman: Yes. Could you make sure the housekeeping note still says I want service later? I do not want the afternoon team to think I refused everything.
  13. Nico: It does. This covers ordinary housekeeping visits; the hotel's separate emergency procedures are unchanged.
  14. Ms Rahman: Understood. I am asking about routine service, not emergencies. Please let me know if the afternoon team cannot fit me in.
  15. Nico: I will. I have recorded the request, but I still need their answer before I can say it is arranged.
  16. Ms Rahman: Please send me the confirmation once you have it. A recorded request has been mistaken for a booking on another stay.
  17. Nico: Certainly. We can check tomorrow's arrangements separately when you know what you would like.
  18. Ms Rahman: That suits me. I may want a different time window tomorrow, but I have not decided. Please leave tomorrow's timing open for now.
  19. Nico: To confirm today's request: no routine visit before two, housekeeping afterward if available, and no change requested for the remaining days.
  20. Ms Rahman: Correct. That is a clear, factual note. Thank you for correcting it. I will wait for your message about the afternoon.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Ask before interpreting a declined service

Fictional staff coaching: a guest declines luggage assistance; a porter attributes the choice to nationality, although the guest gave no reason.

  1. Porter: The guest declined luggage assistance. People from that country probably prefer carrying everything themselves.
  2. Guest services supervisor: We don't know that. What did the guest actually say when you offered the service?
  3. Porter: Only 'No, thank you.' They didn't explain why they preferred not to use it.
  4. Guest services supervisor: Then respect the choice without adding a cultural explanation to the guest profile.
  5. Porter: Should I ask them why they refused, so we can improve our service standard?
  6. Guest services supervisor: There's no need to press for a reason. Ask about future assistance only if it's useful.
  7. Porter: I could say, 'Would you like us to leave you to settle in, or help later?'
  8. Guest services supervisor: Yes, provided it doesn't become repeated pressure after they've clearly declined assistance.
  9. Porter: I'll record only a relevant stated preference, not a guess about nationality or personality.
  10. Guest services supervisor: Good. Our service can respond to the individual without treating an assumption as a cultural expectation.
A recovery gesture needs a preference check

Separate fictional case: a hotel may offer flowers or a fruit basket as an approved recovery gesture; neither has been selected for a corporate traveler.

  1. Guest relations coordinator: We can offer flowers or a fruit basket after the room mix-up. Which should we send?
  2. Duty manager: Have we asked the traveler whether they would welcome either recovery gesture in their room?
  3. Guest relations coordinator: No. I assumed flowers would be more appropriate based on the guest's profile photograph.
  4. Duty manager: A photograph doesn't tell us that preference. We should offer the options without making that assumption.
  5. Guest relations coordinator: Could asking spoil the gesture and make the apology feel less personal to them?
  6. Duty manager: It can be considerate. Explain the options briefly and make clear that declining is perfectly acceptable.
  7. Guest relations coordinator: I'll ask, 'Would either option be welcome, or would you prefer no delivery?'
  8. Duty manager: That works. Don't infer dietary preferences from the guest's name when discussing the fruit basket.
  9. Guest relations coordinator: I'll confirm the choice directly and record only what is needed for this delivery.
  10. Duty manager: Then the gesture supports the apology rather than imposing our idea of good service.
Formal or first-name introductions

Separate fictional case: an event host asks a hotel team how to address an international delegation; delegates have not provided individual preferences.

  1. Event host: Should the reception team use first names for everyone? The delegation is attending an informal retreat.
  2. Front office trainer: Informal describes the event, but it doesn't confirm every delegate's preferred form of address.
  3. Event host: We have names on the rooming list. Could we choose a style based on their countries?
  4. Front office trainer: That would substitute a national assumption for an individual preference we can ask about respectfully.
  5. Event host: How can the team ask without turning each check-in into an awkward interview?
  6. Front office trainer: A simple 'How would you prefer us to address you?' allows a direct answer.
  7. Event host: And if someone doesn't express a preference, should we invent one for the guest profile?
  8. Front office trainer: No. Follow our service standard while keeping any unconfirmed preference out of the record.
  9. Event host: I'll brief the team to ask naturally and use the answer consistently during the stay.
  10. Front office trainer: Good. Clarifying a preference is more reliable than trying to predict it from a nationality.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A timing preference is not a nationality-based rule
  1. Read the completed script, giving the guest time to correct the whole-stay assumption.
  2. Repeat turns 11-16, distinguishing the recorded request from the service confirmation.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A one-day request stays a one-day request

A guest asks for no routine service before 15:00 today and requests later service if available. The team has not confirmed a slot. No preference has been stated for tomorrow.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Agent: "The no-entry boundary is before ___ today."

Answer and explanation

A. 15:00

The guest supplied fifteen hundred as today's routine-entry boundary.

2. Guest: "Later service is subject to ___."

Answer and explanation

D. availability

The team has not yet confirmed that a suitable later slot exists.

3. Agent: "Tomorrow's preference remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unstated

No request has been supplied for the following day.

4. Guest: "Please record my individual request without a cultural ___."

Answer and explanation

A. stereotype

A nationality-based generalization is not evidence of this guest's service preference.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.
